--- SENTENCE 58 ---
Pago Pago Harbor showing the Aua Line stations at which corals were grown, and depths of borings through the reefs at Utelei and Aua.

#

--- SENTENCE 59 ---
SOME CORALS FROM AMERICAN SAMOA AND THE FIJI ISLANDS.

--- SENTENCE 60 ---
By J. Edward Hoffmeister,

Assistant Professor of Geology at the University of Rochester.

### INTRODUCTION.

--- SENTENCE 61 ---
HISTORICAL REVIEW OF RECENT INVESTIGATIONS OF THE DEPARTMENT ON CORALS AND CORAL-REEF PROBLEMS.

--- SENTENCE 62 ---
For a number of years the Department of Marine Biology of the Carnegie Institution of Washington, under the direction of the late Dr. Alfred G. Mayor, has done considerable work on the study of coral reefs and problems relating to them.

--- SENTENCE 63 ---
The investigations were first undertaken at the Department's laboratory at Tortugas, Florida, on the corals along the Florida reef-tract.

--- SENTENCE 64 ---
In 1908, Dr. T. Wayland Vaughan began a study of the corals of this area, the results of which important work have been published in Year Books Nos.

--- SENTENCE 65 ---
7 to 14 of the Carnegie Institution of Washington and elsewhere.

--- SENTENCE 66 ---
The large number of problems and unsettled questions originated by these inquiries naturally led the investigators into other fields in search for solutions and verifications.

--- SENTENCE 67 ---
In 1913 an expedition set out for Thursday Island in Torres Straits, off the northern end of Cape York, in order to study the celebrated coral reefs there.

--- SENTENCE 68 ---
Much to the disappointment of the members, however, the reefs were found to be covered by a layer of mud which killed all but the largest corals.

--- SENTENCE 69 ---
The expedition then headed for the Murray Islands, 120 miles from Thursday Island, within 5 miles of the outer edge of the Great Barrier Reef and 75 miles south of the coast of New Guinea.

--- SENTENCE 70 ---
Here rich coral reefs were found, with abundant opportunity for study.

--- SENTENCE 71 ---
Due to the illness of Dr. Vaughan, which prevented him from accompanying the expedition.

--- SENTENCE 72 ---
Dr. Mayor undertook a study of the ecology of the Murray Island reefs.

--- SENTENCE 73 ---
The former, however, critically worked over a large collection of corals from there, together with collections from Cocos-Keeling Islands and Fanning Island.

--- SENTENCE 74 ---
The results of these investigations are to be found in Publication No. 213, Carnegie Institution of Washington.

#### THE SAMOAN EXPEDITION.

--- SENTENCE 75 ---
Following this, the next region to be studied was the island of Tutuila, American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 76 ---
The investigation here lasted over a number of years.

--- SENTENCE 77 ---
The exact time extended over the following periods: March 4 to April 18, 1917; July 1 to August 5, 1918; July 21 to September 17, 1919; April 2 to July 28, 1920, excepting the time from April 6 to May 14, which was devoted to a visit to Fiji, where a collection of corals was made, which are considered in this paper.

--- SENTENCE 78 ---
The corals collected at Samoa, together with the few specimens from the Fiji Islands, were sent to Dr. Vaughan at the U. S. National Museum.

--- SENTENCE 79 ---
On

the kind recommendation of Professor Edward W. Berry, of the Johns Hopkins University, I was invited by Dr. Vaughan to study them and prepare the report which forms the subject-matter of this paper.

--- SENTENCE 80 ---
The task has proved to be a very interesting and profitable one to me, and it is my hope that in some small measure the results obtained may add to the general knowledge of the coral fauna.

--- SENTENCE 81 ---
Advantages Enjoyed in the Performance of the Work.

--- SENTENCE 82 ---
The conditions under which the work has been accomplished have been ideal.

--- SENTENCE 83 ---
Through the kindness of Dr. R. S. Bassler, of the U. S. National Museum, I have had the use of an excellent room at that institution, where the facilities for such a study as this are unparalleled.

--- SENTENCE 84 ---
Here I have had the privilege of communicating at all times with Dr. Vaughan, who has always graciously and freely given of his time and advice.

--- SENTENCE 85 ---
Dr. Vaughan placed at my disposal all the coral collections in the museum, and also permitted me the use of his books.

--- SENTENCE 86 ---
The collection of living species of corals in the U. S. National Museum is probably exceeded in size only by that of the British Museum (Natural History).

--- SENTENCE 87 ---
The important collections which it contains include the following:

(1) That of the U. S. Exploring Expedition, which Dana studied and reported on in his volume on Zoophytes.

--- SENTENCE 88 ---
The Museum contains most of the type species described by him.

--- SENTENCE 89 ---
(2) That of the North Pacific Exploring Expedition, described by Dr. E. A.

Verrill, of Yale University.

--- SENTENCE 90 ---
(3) The important collections made by the U. S. Bureau of Fisheries steamer Albatross, especially that made in the Hawaiian Islands in 1902, which furnished the basis for Dr. Vaughan's work on "Madreporaria of the Hawaiian Islands and Laysan," Bulletin 59, U. S. National Museum.

--- SENTENCE 91 ---
(4) The Philippine collections, which include about 3,000 specimens purchased from Mr. J. B. Steere, those made by the Albatross in 1907-1908, and other minor

onec

(5) The important collections from Murray Island by Dr. A. G. Mayor, Cocos-Keeling Island by Dr. Wood Jones, and Fanning Island by Mr. Carl Elschner, all of which have been mentioned above.

--- SENTENCE 92 ---
Besides the main collections there are numerous smaller ones, so that taken *en masse* a large store of material for comparative purposes has been available.

--- SENTENCE 93 ---
THE ISLAND OF TUTUILA, WITH AN ACCOUNT OF ITS HISTORY.

--- SENTENCE 94 ---
The island of Tutuila is the largest of the group known as American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 95 ---
The others consists of Aunuu and Cockscomb, which are satellites of Tutuila, and the Manua group, consisting of Tau, Olosega, and Ofu, and Rose Atoll.

--- SENTENCE 96 ---
All of these islands, with the exception of Rose Atoll, are of volcanic origin.

--- SENTENCE 97 ---
Most of the investigations centered about Tutuila, and by far the greater part of the corals were collected from its fringing reef.

--- SENTENCE 98 ---
The harbor

of Pago Pago, with its rich coral reefs, offered an ideal location for experiments of this nature.

--- SENTENCE 99 ---
The geology of Tutuila was investigated by Professors Reginald A. Daly and Rollin T. Chamberlin.

--- SENTENCE 100 ---
The results of these investigations may be found in the annual report of the Director of the Department for the years 1919 and 1920 respectively, and also in Publication 340 of the Carnegie Institution of Washington.

--- SENTENCE 101 ---
The latter work gives a thorough discussion of the problem.

--- SENTENCE 102 ---
Since one of the main objects of the expedition was to obtain more knowledge on the old question of the formation of coral reefs, particular attention was given to this phase of the subject.

--- SENTENCE 103 ---
Dr. Mayor (Annual Report, 1920) sums up the history of Tutuila as conceived by Dr. Chamberlin as follows:

"A platform about 2 miles in width was cut by marine erosion around the island.

--- SENTENCE 104 ---
Then this platform became submerged, while at the same time the island tilted so that the platform sank to a slightly greater depth on the southeast than along the north shore.

--- SENTENCE 105 ---
Then reefs grew around the island, and on the north shore, where the platform was shallow, the fringing reefs largely fused with the off-shore barrier reef; but on the south shore, where the platform was submerged more deeply, the fringing reef in most places did not fuse with the barrier reef, but the lagoon remained intact between them.

--- SENTENCE 106 ---
Later a submergence of about 190 feet occurred and the sea cliffed the shores, and finally the sea-level sank about 10 feet and then the modern fringing reefs began to grow outward from the shores over the seaward slopes of the island.

--- SENTENCE 107 ---
"

On the outer margin of the old 2-mile-wide platform surrounding the island corals began to form a barrier reef.

--- SENTENCE 108 ---
At present this reef is submerged about 180 feet and the platform on which it grew is at least 400 feet below the present sea-level.

--- SENTENCE 109 ---
Thus, according to Chamberlin (Annual Report, 1920):

"Tutuila, therefore, is consistent with the Darwin-Dana coral-reef hypothesis to the extent that a submergence of 400 feet has occurred since the corals began to form the old barrier reef; but in other respects it does not fit the requirements of that hypothesis, inasmuch as the barrier reef, instead of being built up several thousand feet from the slopes of a sinking island, is found to be rooted on a broad, wave-cut platform which, slightly submerged, afforded favorable conditions for coral-reef growth."

Chamberlin's explanation of "planation plus subsidence" is thoroughly treated in Publication 340.

--- SENTENCE 110 ---
Some of the Methods Used in the Investigation and a Few of the Results Obtained.

--- SENTENCE 111 ---
In order to facilitate the work of systematically studying the relations and abundance of the different species of corals on the reef-flat of Pago Pago Harbor, a line was surveyed across the reef.

--- SENTENCE 112 ---
This line, known as the Aua line because of its nearness to the village of Aua, was 855 feet long.

--- SENTENCE 113 ---
It was surveyed and marked at 100-foot intervals by iron stakes across the reef-flat off the southern end of Aua village, on the east side of Pago Pago Harbor.

--- SENTENCE 114 ---
<sup>&</sup>lt;sup>1</sup> See map of Pago Pago Harbor, plate A.

The line starts from a large "Pua tree" on the beach and runs S. 39° W. (magnetic) to the largest coral rock on the outer edge of the reef, marked "coral block, 3 feet," on Hydrographic Chart No. 2563, of Pago Pago Harbor.

--- SENTENCE 115 ---
Dr. Mayor carried out many experiments over the reef-flat to determine the growth-rate, the influence of changed habitat, silt, fresh water, etc., on various species of corals.

--- SENTENCE 116 ---
The detailed results of these investigations appear in his two papers, "The Structure and Ecology of the Samoan Coral Reefs" and "The Growth-rate of Samoan Corals" (Publication 340, Carnegie Institution of Washington).

--- SENTENCE 117 ---
Often the experiment of dividing a colony from the reef-flat in half and placing one part in shallow, agitated water and the other half in deep, quiet water, was carried out.

--- SENTENCE 118 ---
In this paper I have endeavored to point out the differences which these opposite conditions have caused on the morphology of the corals.

--- SENTENCE 119 ---
At Tutuila, just as at Murray Island, Australia, the largest number of corals was found to be growing in comparatively quiet water, about 200 feet shoreward from the region where the surges die out.

--- SENTENCE 120 ---
The largest number of species, however, was found where the surges die out in ordinary weather.

--- SENTENCE 121 ---
Table 1 illustrates the percentage of coral-heads of the four genera which comprise over 90 per cent of the colonies of the reef-flats, as reported by Dr. Mayor.

--- SENTENCE 122 ---
TABLE I.

| Name of coral.

--- SENTENCE 123 ---
| Percentage o   | f coral heads.

--- SENTENCE 124 ---

| Porites        | 10<br>rare     | p. ct.<br>47.4

--- SENTENCE 125 ---
| 91             | 95.01                                         |

From this it is seen that *Porites* is the most abundant genus at the two places, and yet, according to Dr. Mayor, it is not so conspicuous as *Acropora*.

--- SENTENCE 126 ---
At Samoa, *Psammocora* takes the place of *Seriatopora*, which is entirely absent.

#### CLASSIFICATION OF THE LARGER CORAL GROUPS.

--- SENTENCE 127 ---
Concerning the present system of classification, I can only repeat after others that it is in a highly unsatisfactory condition.

--- SENTENCE 128 ---
As Dr. Vaughan has pointed out in his Murray Island paper, there are certain groups which are apparently natural, such as the Fungiidæ, the Acroporidæ, and the Poritidæ.

--- SENTENCE 129 ---
Such a group, however, as the so-called Astræidæ present such problems that it is very difficult to classify them.

--- SENTENCE 130 ---
Mr. Matthai's attempt to

<sup>1</sup> Matthai, G., 1914.

--- SENTENCE 131 ---
London, 2d ser., Zool., vol. 17, pt. 1, pp.

--- SENTENCE 132 ---
1-140, 38 pls.

classify them on the basis of the character of the directive mesenteries has turned out to be very unsatisfactory, it seems to me, when it is compelled to include within the genus Favia many species which, in my opinion, are very different generically.

--- SENTENCE 133 ---
Dr. Vaughan's treatment of the group (Car.

--- SENTENCE 134 ---
Wash., Pub. 213, p. 100) appears to me to be much more satisfactory, and I am following his method in this paper.

--- SENTENCE 135 ---
He divides Matthai's genus Favia into three families, Orbicellidæ, Faviidæ, and Mussidæ, and distributes the species among the four genera Orbicella, Favia, Favites, and Acanthastrea.

--- SENTENCE 136 ---
The main criteria on which this classification is based is the method of asexual reproduction and the character of the septal margins.

--- SENTENCE 137 ---
Dr. Vaughan emphasizes the tentativeness of this classification and realizes its weakness.

#### PROBLEM OF THE SPECIES.

--- SENTENCE 138 ---
The species problem, difficult as it is in all biologic groups, is still more perplexing in such variable forms of life as the corals, where environment stamps its influence so effectively.

--- SENTENCE 139 ---
With our insufficient knowledge concerning the evolution of species, we can only use the term "species" in a more or less artificial manner for purposes of classification.

--- SENTENCE 140 ---
Our main object, it seems to me, with our present information, should be, therefore, to give as clear a picture as possible of the material on which we are working, realizing the imperfection of our classification.

--- SENTENCE 141 ---
With this thought uppermost, we can use to advantage the term "species" as defined by Dr. Vaughan in his report of the Hawaiian Island corals.

--- SENTENCE 142 ---
He says:

"A species is a group of individuals connected among themselves by intergrading characters and separated by distinct lacunæ from all other individuals or groups of individuals.

--- SENTENCE 143 ---
"

If our knowledge of corals were greater, and if we knew the limits of each coral species in its evolutionary march, either continuous or discontinuous, then we could use the Linnæan system as it stands without trouble.

--- SENTENCE 144 ---
It is at times, however, very difficult to know the limits of these species, since the variation is so great.

--- SENTENCE 145 ---
Ordinarily, if we think a specimen is far enough removed from a recognized and described type to suit our own personal views, we give it another specific name.

--- SENTENCE 146 ---
Another person may not think it far enough removed to justify its separation.

--- SENTENCE 147 ---
Bernard (The Unit of Classification for Systematic Biology, Proc.

--- SENTENCE 148 ---
Soc., vol. 11, 1901, pp.

--- SENTENCE 149 ---
268-280) clearly recognized the limits of the Linnæan system as applied to our present knowledge of corals and experienced such difficulty that he proposed a temporary abandonment of it, with the substitution of a locality-number system.

--- SENTENCE 150 ---
He proposed keeping the generic name and putting in place of the specific name a numerical fraction which told the number of distinct forms of the genus at that locality and to which one of these forms the specimen happened to belong.

--- SENTENCE 151 ---
Thus, "Porites, Singapore, \frac{4}{20}" would mean that there were 20 distinct forms of

Porites known from Singapore, and that the one referred to was described and figured as No. 4.

--- SENTENCE 152 ---
Bernard used this system in cataloguing the perforate corals of the British Museum.

--- SENTENCE 153 ---
Mr. J. Stanley Gardiner (On the Unit of Classification for Systematic Biology; A Reply to Mr. Bernard.

--- SENTENCE 154 ---
Soc., vol. 11, 1902, pp.

--- SENTENCE 155 ---
423-427) criticized Mr. Bernard's proposal severely, mainly because of the unit of his classification, and also for the fact that it takes into account locality alone.

--- SENTENCE 156 ---
I can thoroughly appreciate Bernard's attitude toward the binomial system, and yet do not believe that he gave us a better plan.

--- SENTENCE 157 ---
Even the fact that it is a numerical classification is against it.

--- SENTENCE 158 ---
It gives no picture of the species, an essential part of any classification.

--- SENTENCE 159 ---
Ideas can much more readily be remembered when associated with a name than with a number.

--- SENTENCE 160 ---
The average person does not react to a number as he does to a name, about which there is something distinctive.

--- SENTENCE 161 ---
Gardiner, himself, believes that he can recognize the limits of species so that he can use the binomial system to advantage.

--- SENTENCE 162 ---
He admits, however, that the separation of species from varieties requires "the examination of thousands of specimens, and that it is a task of Herculean proportions.

--- SENTENCE 163 ---
"

When a large number of specimens are available the variation may be seen to be enormous in some species.

--- SENTENCE 164 ---
This is well illustrated in such species of Pavona as are described below.

--- SENTENCE 165 ---
When there is a slight gradation taking place through many forms, so that the specimen which represents one end of the series and the one which represents the other end appear to be specifically unlike, we are still obliged, in accordance with our definition, to place them all in the same species.

--- SENTENCE 166 ---
Often we find that two apparently distinct species are in reality members of a series connected by intergrading characters.

--- SENTENCE 167 ---
Frequently we find specimens that lie between two previously described species and thus indicate the possibility that all may belong to an intergrading series.

--- SENTENCE 168 ---
The question is, how are we going to designate these midway specimens?

--- SENTENCE 169 ---
Shall they be given a new specific name?

--- SENTENCE 170 ---
This problem has arisen several times in connection with this work, and I have often been tempted to solve it in the manner outlined below.

--- SENTENCE 171 ---
Suppose we find some specimens which combine some of the characters of two previously described species and appear to lie midway between them.

--- SENTENCE 172 ---
Ordinarily we treat them in one of the following ways: We either give them a new specific name; call them a variety under one or the other outlying species; or else say that they are still within the limits of that species.

--- SENTENCE 173 ---
Instead of following any one of the methods, it seems to me that the simplest manner of treatment would be to use some symbol which would tell our opinion in the matter and at the same time serve to locate as accurately as possible the position of the specimens in the nomenclatural table.

--- SENTENCE 174 ---
If, for instance, we are undecided concerning some specimens, since they combine some of the characters of Leptoria gracilis (Dana) and some of Leptoria phrygia (Ellis and Solander), and if they appear closer to the latter, we might easily designate them

Leptoria phrygia (Ellis and Solander)-gracilis (Dana).

--- SENTENCE 175 ---
This reads

Leptoria phrygia (Ellis and Solander) grade gracilis (Dana).

--- SENTENCE 176 ---
Following this it would be necessary to make a clear statement as to the reason for the designation, making plain which characters are variable.

--- SENTENCE 177 ---
This not only gives a picture of the specimens, but I believe, if the method were followed to any extent, it would aid materially toward the collection of information showing in what direction the march of evolutionary variation tends.

--- SENTENCE 178 ---
I have been somewhat hesitant about using this system to any great extent here until I have had more expressions of opinion from others concerning its value.

--- SENTENCE 179 ---
In his publications, Dr. Vaughan has endeavored to make clear the forms which he has described by an extensive use of the term "variety."

--- SENTENCE 180 ---
He says in his Hawaiian paper: "A variety would be a secondary mode on a species curve."

--- SENTENCE 181 ---
Wherever possible I have made use of this system, which often meets the requirements to the best advantage.

#### SAMOAN CORALS ACCORDING TO STATION.

--- SENTENCE 182 ---
The stations from which Dr. Mayor collected the Samoan corals come under four distinct divisions.

--- SENTENCE 183 ---
First, those which were taken at measured distances from shore along the Aua line; second, those from various localities on the Aua reef-patch without regard to the Aua line; third, those dredged at various places in and near Pago Pago Harbor; fourth, those from tide-pools near the seaward edge of the Aua reef and the Utelei reef.

--- SENTENCE 184 ---
Tables 2 to 6 inclusive give the lists of species and other information of these divisions in the order indicated above.

A certain amount of interpolation was resorted to in computing these figures.

--- SENTENCE 269 ---

--- SENTENCE 271 ---

--- SENTENCE 273 ---
The line I across the southeast reef of Murray Island, which is equivalent to the Aua line, begins at 300 feet from shore and reaches to the seaward edge of the reef at 1,775 feet from shore.

--- SENTENCE 274 ---
Table 3 shows that there are a few corals of massive growth-form growing within 50 to 100 feet of the shore, but, as in the case of the Australian corals, they reach their greatest development near the outer edge of the reef, between 700 and 800 feet.

--- SENTENCE 275 ---
Those with stout branches begin near the center of the reef and increase in the number of species toward the outer edge.

--- SENTENCE 276 ---
Those with fragile branches grow best in the central portion of the reef and are reduced in number near the outer and inner edges.

--- SENTENCE 315 ---
Aua reef and Utelei reef are on opposite sides of Pago Pago Harbor.

--- SENTENCE 316 ---
The distance between the seaward edge of one from that of the other is about 3,000 feet.

--- SENTENCE 317 ---
The list in table 6 gives the names of those species found growing in the tide-pools on the seaward edges of these reefs.

--- SENTENCE 318 ---
There are 40 species in all, and 16 of these are common to both reefs.

Pocillopora damicornis Milne Edwards and Haime, Coralliaries, tome 3, p. 303.

1918.

--- SENTENCE 511 ---
Pocillopora bulbosa Vaughan, Carnegie Inst.

--- SENTENCE 512 ---
Wash. Pub. 213, p. 75, plate 21, figs.

--- SENTENCE 513 ---
1, 1a; plate 12, figs.

--- SENTENCE 514 ---
This is a much confused case of nomenclature.

--- SENTENCE 515 ---
To begin with, Linnæus described a species under the name of *Millepora damicornis*

--- SENTENCE 516 ---
(S. N., ed. 10, sp. 9).

--- SENTENCE 517 ---
Pallas (1766) placed it under *Madrepora damicornis* and recognized three varieties which he called  $\alpha$ ,  $\beta$ , and  $\gamma$ .

--- SENTENCE 518 ---
Following this, Ellis and Solander (1786, Hist.

--- SENTENCE 519 ---
Zooph., p. 170, No. 73) redescribed the  $\gamma$  variety of Pallas.

--- SENTENCE 520 ---
In his "Die Pflanzenthiere" Esper described four so-called varieties of M. damicornis and published four plates (Nos. 46, 46A, 47, and 48) illustrating these varieties.

--- SENTENCE 521 ---
He considered the variety illustrated by plate 46 as representing the  $\beta$  variety of Pallas, plate 46A the  $\gamma$  variety, and plate 48 equivalent to the  $\alpha$  variety.

--- SENTENCE 522 ---
In 1816, Lamarck revised the classification and separated the genus *Pocillo*pora from *Madrepora*.

--- SENTENCE 523 ---
He believed that Esper's plates 46 and 46A illustrate P. damicornis, but referred to them as describing the  $\alpha$  and  $\beta$  varieties of Pallas and gave the name P. acuta to the  $\gamma$  variety.

--- SENTENCE 524 ---
Ehrenberg (1834, Genus LXXV, sp. 3) follows Lamarck and describes a new species, *Pocillopora bulbosa*.

--- SENTENCE 525 ---
Dana (1846) considers *P. damicornis* as belonging to the alpha variety of Pallas and to Esper's plate 47.

--- SENTENCE 526 ---
He believes *P. bulbosa*

--- SENTENCE 527 ---
Ehrenberg is represented by Esper's plate 46, and believes plate 46A represents a variety of the same species.

--- SENTENCE 528 ---
Milne Edwards and Haime (1860) agree with Dana in referring *P. bulbosa* to Esper's plate 46, but believe that *P. damicornis* is represented by plate 46A.

--- SENTENCE 529 ---
In all this confusion one fact seems to stand out.

--- SENTENCE 530 ---
It is noticeable that the majority of writers have in mind primarily the same idea as to the typical *P. damicornis*.

--- SENTENCE 531 ---
Ellis and Solander believe that their *P. damicornis* is equivalent to the gamma variety of Pallas, which according to Esper is illustrated by his plate

15

46A. Lamarck refers to 46 and 46A as describing P. damicornis.

--- SENTENCE 532 ---
Milne Edwards and Haime agree that the typical P. damicornis is represented by Esper's plate 46A. Dana alone disagrees when he refers to damicornis as represented by Esper's plate 47.

--- SENTENCE 533 ---
Concerning P. bulbosa Ehrenberg, Milne Edwards and Haime and Dana are agreed that it is illustrated by Esper's plate 46.

--- SENTENCE 534 ---
Milne Edwards and Haime say that P. damicornis and P. bulbosa do not appear to be specifically distinct from each other.

--- SENTENCE 535 ---
Considering P. damicornis to be represented by Esper's plate 46A, it seems, therefore, as Vaughan (1918) observed, that the damicornis of Dana should be given a different name.

--- SENTENCE 536 ---
One of Dana's specimens is No. 660 in the U. S. National Museum, and it appears to be specifically distinct from the common conception of damicornis.

--- SENTENCE 537 ---
From the above it would seem that P. bulbosa is a synonym of P. damicornis.

--- SENTENCE 538 ---
In the U. S. National Museum there are two specimens which Dana identified as P. bulbosa.

--- SENTENCE 539 ---
One of them (No. 718 U. S. Nat.

--- SENTENCE 540 ---
Mus.), which he figured (plate 49, figs. 6, 6a), is very similar to Esper's poor illustration 46, and is, according to Dana, typical P. bulbosa.

--- SENTENCE 541 ---
The other one (No. 885 U. S. Nat.

--- SENTENCE 542 ---
Mus.), which Dr. Vaughan illustrated (Carnegie Inst.

--- SENTENCE 543 ---
Wash. Pub. 213, plate 21, figs.

--- SENTENCE 544 ---
1, 1a) and which is similar to Esper's plate 46A, is, according to Dana, simply a variety of P. bulbosa.

--- SENTENCE 545 ---
It is quite evident that they are very similar.

--- SENTENCE 546 ---
Since it is necessary to come to some understanding concerning these two and other closely related forms, I believe I have sufficient ground to offer the following classification:

#### Pocillopora damicornis (Linnæus), typical.

--- SENTENCE 547 ---
This is illustrated by Esper's plate 46A. Vaughan's P. bulbosa Ehrenberg (Carnegie Inst.

--- SENTENCE 548 ---
Wash. Pub. 213, plate 21, figs.

--- SENTENCE 549 ---
1, 1a) is also referred here.

--- SENTENCE 550 ---
bulbosa Ehrenberg = P. bulbosa Ehrenberg, typical.

--- SENTENCE 551 ---
This variety is represented by Esper's plate 46.

--- SENTENCE 552 ---
Plate 1, figure 1, of this paper shows Dana's figured specimen of P. bulbosa, which comes here.

#### Pocillopora damicornis var.

--- SENTENCE 553 ---
cespitosa Dana=P. cespitosa Dana.

--- SENTENCE 554 ---
This is the P. cespitosa of Dana.

--- SENTENCE 555 ---
I have examined many specimens of cespitosa, including Dana's type, and have no hesitancy in referring it to a variety to P. damicornis.

--- SENTENCE 556 ---
The only difference between the two is that the former is a neater and a more finely branching type.

--- SENTENCE 557 ---
The typical damicornis has a more straggly appearance and generally thicker branches, which are not so finely divided at the summit into little short branchlets.

--- SENTENCE 558 ---
One variety grades gently into the other.

--- SENTENCE 559 ---
cespitosa is thoroughly described by Vaughan (1907) in his report of the Recent Madreporaria of the Hawaiian Islands and Laysan (U. S. Nat.

--- SENTENCE 560 ---

#### Leptastrea purpurea (Dana).

1846.

--- SENTENCE 742 ---
Astrae purpurea Dana, U. S. Expl.

--- SENTENCE 743 ---
Exped., Zooph., p. 239, plate 12, figs.

--- SENTENCE 744 ---
10, 10a to 10c.

--- SENTENCE 745 ---
Leptastrea ehrenbergiana Milne Edwards and Haime, Ann.

--- SENTENCE 746 ---
3d ser., Zool., vol. 12, p. 120.

--- SENTENCE 747 ---
Leptastrea ehrenbergana Matthai, Trans.

--- SENTENCE 748 ---
London, 2d ser., Zool., vol. 17, p. 68, plate 17,

figs.

--- SENTENCE 749 ---
5 to 7; plate 18, figs.

--- SENTENCE 750 ---
2 and 7; plate 19, figs.

--- SENTENCE 751 ---
3 and 4; plate 34, fig.

--- SENTENCE 752 ---
Leptastrea purpurea Vaughan, Carnegie Inst.

--- SENTENCE 753 ---
Wash. Pub. 213, p. 91, plate 30, figs.

--- SENTENCE 754 ---
1, 1a, 2, 3, 3a; plate 17, fig.

--- SENTENCE 755 ---
There are a large number of specimens belonging to this species in the collection.

--- SENTENCE 756 ---
They are all small, incrusting forms, growing either on lithothamnion or dead coral.

--- SENTENCE 757 ---
The largest corallum is nodular in shape, with a diameter of about 40 mm.

--- SENTENCE 758 ---
The calices vary from 2 to 5 mm. in diameter.

--- SENTENCE 759 ---
They are polygonal and usually have a narrow groove on the summits of the walls.

--- SENTENCE 760 ---
The small calices have 24 septa alternately larger and smaller, all of the larger ones reaching the columella.

--- SENTENCE 761 ---
The largest calice has 52 septa.

--- SENTENCE 762 ---
In medium-sized calices the members of the first and second cycles are practically the same size.

--- SENTENCE 763 ---
As a rule the inner edges of the septa slope downward to the columella.

--- SENTENCE 764 ---
However, in many cases the inner edges are nearly subvertical.

--- SENTENCE 765 ---
The septal faces are possessed with coarse, conical granulations.

--- SENTENCE 766 ---
The septal edges become more dentate as they approach the columella.

--- SENTENCE 767 ---
The latter is largely papillate and composed of fused edges of the dentate septa.

--- SENTENCE 768 ---
Reproduction takes place predominantly by interstitial budding.

--- SENTENCE 769 ---
Concerning the differences between L. purpurea and L. transversa, Vaughan (op.

--- SENTENCE 770 ---
cit., p. 90) says,

"Two of the species, L. purpurea and L. transversa, have conspicuously polygonal corallites and calices (which are often deformed) and no free corallite limbs.

--- SENTENCE 771 ---
In the former the grouping of the higher cycles of septa around the lower is usually obvious.

--- SENTENCE 772 ---
The septal edges are conspicuously dentate, the septal faces show distinct granulations, and the columella is papillate.

--- SENTENCE 773 ---
In the latter the septal grouping is rare, the septal edges are entire or only with microscopically fine dentations, the septal faces are nearly smooth, and the columella is either without distinct papillae or they are only slightly developed.

--- SENTENCE 774 ---
Usually there is a lamella extending lengthwise of the calice between the directive septa, while the principal septa outside the directive plane fuse to its sides."

#### Favia favus (Forskål).

--- SENTENCE 868 ---
Favia favus Matthai, Trans.

--- SENTENCE 869 ---
London., 2d ser., Zool., vol. 17, p. 79, plate 9, fig.

--- SENTENCE 870 ---
2; plate 20, figs.

I to 8; plate 22, figs.

--- SENTENCE 873 ---
I to 5; plate 32, fig.

--- SENTENCE 874 ---
I.

I am referring 6 specimens to this species.

--- SENTENCE 875 ---
Corallum forming rather light, convex clumps.

--- SENTENCE 876 ---
Corallites elliptical to polygonal, often deformed.

--- SENTENCE 877 ---
The calices are somewhat smaller than those of Matthai's specimens and the number of septa correspondingly less.

--- SENTENCE 878 ---
Average size 10 by 7 mm. across and 6.5 mm.

--- SENTENCE 879 ---
Distance between corallites averages 1 to 1.5 mm.

Number of septa in average-sized calice is 30, of which 9 or 10 reach columella.

--- SENTENCE 880 ---
They are rather thick and are exsert about 1 mm.

--- SENTENCE 881 ---
Each septum may be divided into two parts: (1) an upper portion which extends from the corallite rim to about half the way to the columella; (2) at this point it widens and forms a bench of 1 mm., and then drops nearly vertically to the columella.

--- SENTENCE 882 ---
The serrations of the septal margins are large on the upper portion and much finer and more numerous on the lower.

--- SENTENCE 883 ---
In 2 specimens the septa of the first cycle are very prominent in some of the calices.

--- SENTENCE 884 ---
Septal faces covered with rough spinules, which, with the exsert, serrated margins, give the corallum a rough appearance throughout.

--- SENTENCE 885 ---
The columella is deep seated and rather poorly developed.

--- SENTENCE 886 ---

#### Favia stelligera (Dana).

figs.

--- SENTENCE 899 ---
1, 1a, 2, 2a, 3, 4. (With synonymy).

--- SENTENCE 900 ---
Corallum heavy; texture dense.

--- SENTENCE 901 ---
Often formed like slightly flattened cylinder with all edges rounded off.

--- SENTENCE 902 ---
Surface smooth to gently undulating.

--- SENTENCE 903 ---
The individual corallites are much smaller than those of the other species of the genus.

--- SENTENCE 904 ---
They average from 2 to 2.5 mm.

--- SENTENCE 905 ---
in diameter; usually subcircular except when elongated for reproduction; rims distinct.

--- SENTENCE 906 ---
Distance between corallites ranges from 0.75 to 2 mm.; average distance I mm.

--- SENTENCE 907 ---
Number of septa ranges from 15 to 24; 6 to 13 meet columella.

--- SENTENCE 908 ---
Matthai speaks of them thickening towards walls.

--- SENTENCE 909 ---
This thickening is so slight that it is scarcely noticeable in the Samoan specimens.

--- SENTENCE 910 ---
The septa are roughly serrated and covered with fine spinules.

--- SENTENCE 911 ---
They are slightly exsert and are separated by a groove from those of adjoining corallites.

--- SENTENCE 912 ---
A cycle of very small septa usually alternate with the well-developed ones.

--- SENTENCE 913 ---
The columella is distinct, but very loosely constructed.

--- SENTENCE 914 ---
Reproduction takes place usually by subequal fission, but there are many cases of distinctly unequal fission.

--- SENTENCE 915 ---
A partition never crosses the septa.

--- SENTENCE 916 ---

water in shallow tide-pools near seaward edge of Aua Reef.

#### Favia rotumana (Gardiner).

--- SENTENCE 947 ---
3a, 3b, 3c.

1899.

--- SENTENCE 948 ---

--- SENTENCE 949 ---
London, 2d ser., Zool., vol. 17, p. 113, sp. 23.

--- SENTENCE 950 ---
The two fragments from Rose Island have a rather dense, solid structure, and the one from the Utelei Reef is incrusting lithothamnion.

--- SENTENCE 951 ---
The following is a brief description of specimen No. 2, from the former locality.

--- SENTENCE 952 ---
This is a piece of what was evidently a large, heavy, subhemispherical corallum.

--- SENTENCE 953 ---
The corallites are subelliptical, except where compressed to distinctly polygonal shape.

--- SENTENCE 954 ---
Average size 10 by 7 mm., and 7 mm.

--- SENTENCE 955 ---
As a rule, they are close

together.

--- SENTENCE 956 ---
Septa thickened in wall; exsert as much as 2.5 mm.; drop nearly vertically into calice, but near bottom they form bench containing palar protuberance, from which the principal ones extend downward to columella.

--- SENTENCE 957 ---
Septal edges may be nearly entire, except for very fine dentations.

--- SENTENCE 958 ---
In the average-sized calice 10 or 11 septa join the columella.

--- SENTENCE 959 ---
Septal faces contain very fine granulations.

--- SENTENCE 960 ---
The columella is made of septal trabeculæ and is very spongy.

--- SENTENCE 961 ---
Reproduction by subequal fission.

--- SENTENCE 962 ---
Besides these specimens, the U. S. National Museum has some splendid examples of the species from Hereheretue Island.

Favites abdita (Ellis and Solander).

--- SENTENCE 977 ---
Mayor's "Growth-Rate of Samoan Corals," plate 7, figs. 20, a, b.

1914.

--- SENTENCE 978 ---
Favia abdita Matthai, Trans.

--- SENTENCE 979 ---
London, 2d ser., Zool., vol. 17, p. 91, plate 9, fig.

--- SENTENCE 980 ---
5; plate 29, figs.

--- SENTENCE 981 ---
1 to 4; plate 35, fig.

--- SENTENCE 982 ---
Favites abdita Vaughan, Carnegie Inst.

--- SENTENCE 983 ---
Wash. Pub. 213, p. 109, plate 40, figs.

--- SENTENCE 984 ---
1 to 5; plate 16, fig. 31.

--- SENTENCE 985 ---
In general, the corallum has a hillocky mode of structure.

--- SENTENCE 986 ---
On the hillocks the corallites slope against one another.

--- SENTENCE 987 ---
Usually the walls are appreciably thicker at the base than at the top and thus resemble waves.

--- SENTENCE 988 ---
The calices are polygonal and very variable in diameter and depth.

--- SENTENCE 989 ---
Averages in these dimensions are about 8 mm.

--- SENTENCE 990 ---
and 4 mm. respectively.

--- SENTENCE 991 ---
The average-sized calices contain 33 septa, not including the cycle of very fine, insignificant ones with nearly entire edges.

--- SENTENCE 992 ---
Septal margins very roughly serrated, the serrations increasing in size towards the columella, although rather prominent at the summit also.

--- SENTENCE 993 ---
At the base they often constitute a palar crown around the columella.

--- SENTENCE 994 ---
The septa are exsert about 1 mm.

--- SENTENCE 995 ---
The columella is composed of

twisted trabeculæ and often has a papillate appearance.

--- SENTENCE 996 ---
Reproduction takes place by marginal fission.

--- SENTENCE 997 ---
I have referred 5 specimens to this species.

--- SENTENCE 998 ---
No. 2 is practically exactly like Dana's type of Astraa robusta, which is nearly typical Favites abdita.

--- SENTENCE 999 ---
The other specimens differ from the typical form by their smaller calices, and also by having rather prominent septal dentations just within the calicular rims, which, however, become larger towards the columella.

--- SENTENCE 1000 ---
Generally the typical F. abdita has septal margins which are fairly smooth near the summit of the wall and become rougher farther down.

--- SENTENCE 1001 ---
Distinguishing characteristics.—The hillocky appearance is in general characteristic of F. abdita, but can not be relied upon too much.

--- SENTENCE 1002 ---
This peculiar growth-form is a result of the method of reproduction, and is therefore liable to be found in all species of Favites.

--- SENTENCE 1003 ---
Some specimens of F. abdita resemble those of F. halicora (Ehrenberg) so closely that it is very hard to tell them apart.

--- SENTENCE 1004 ---
In general, they can be separated by the shallower calices, hillocky structure, and more gently sloping septa of F. abdita.

--- SENTENCE 1005 ---
Usually F. halicora has walls with flatter, often centrally grooved summits, and septa which plunge down sharply into the calicular opening.

--- SENTENCE 1006 ---
As a rule, also, the septal dentations just within the calices of F. halicora are more pronounced than is the case in the other species.

--- SENTENCE 1007 ---
Stations, Pago Pago Harbor, Tutuila, Samoa:

No. 1 = No. 20 (Mayor).

--- SENTENCE 1008 ---
From about 700 feet out on Aua Reef.

--- SENTENCE 1009 ---
Water agitated; bottom hard limestone; 1 foot deep at lowest tides.

--- SENTENCE 1010 ---
Aua line, 600 to 624 feet from shore.

--- SENTENCE 1011 ---
Pure agitated water in shallow tide-pools near seaward edge of Aua Reef.

--- SENTENCE 1012 ---
Aua line, 850 to 874 feet from shore.

--- SENTENCE 1013 ---
Shallow tide-pools on seaward edge of Utelei Reef.

--- SENTENCE 1014 ---
Distribution.—Red Sea; Indian Ocean; widely distributed in Pacific Ocean as far east as Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 1061 ---
The main difference that I am able to make out in all these specimens is in the thickness of the walls.

--- SENTENCE 1062 ---
The Samoan specimens show walls, however, which are as thin as those in typical G. retiformis in some parts of the corallum, and in other parts are as thick as those of Dana's type of G. parvistella.

--- SENTENCE 1063 ---
I am inclined, therefore, to put all of them under Goniastrea retiformis and make the thick-walled specimens a variety, var.

--- SENTENCE 1064 ---
The parvistella variety has slightly smaller calices than those of the type.

--- SENTENCE 1065 ---
I have examined very carefully all of the specimens of G. retiformis in the U.S. National Museum, including those in the Samoan collection, and have compared them with Dana's type of G. parvistella (No. 67 U.S. Nat.

--- SENTENCE 1066 ---
and with Matthai's descriptions and figures of G. solida and G. retiformis.

--- SENTENCE 1067 ---
The following is a brief description of one of the typical specimens:

Corallum massive with even-topped, undulating surface.

--- SENTENCE 1068 ---
Calices polygonal; diameter 2 to 4 mm., depth 1 to 2.5 mm.

--- SENTENCE 1069 ---
Inter-calicular walls are 0.50 to 1 mm. thick.

--- SENTENCE 1070 ---
In an average-sized calice there are 20 septa, alternating with which is a cycle of rudimentary ones.

--- SENTENCE 1071 ---
The well-developed septa unite with the rudimentary ones of the adjoining corallites.

--- SENTENCE 1072 ---
All the septa are roughly serrate.

--- SENTENCE 1073 ---
From 7 to 11 reach the columella, and these have large paliform lobes surrounding it which at times extend two-thirds of the way up to the surface of the corallite.

--- SENTENCE 1074 ---
I am placing all the Samoan specimens with the typical G. retiformis.

--- SENTENCE 1075 ---
Stations, Pago Pago Harbor, Tutuila, Samoa:

No. 1.

--- SENTENCE 1076 ---
Just back of the breakers, in shallow water on Aua Reef flat.

No. 2.

--- SENTENCE 1077 ---
Aua line, 850 to 874 feet from shore.

--- SENTENCE 1078 ---
Pure agitated water in shallow tide-pools near seaward edge of Aua Reef.

--- SENTENCE 1079 ---
Shallow tide-pools on seaward edge of Utelei Reef.

--- SENTENCE 1080 ---

#### Genus LEPTORIA Milne Edwards and Haime.

1848.

--- SENTENCE 1102 ---
Leptoria Milne Edwards and Haime, Acad.

--- SENTENCE 1103 ---
Sci., Comptes rend., vol.

--- SENTENCE 1104 ---
Type species: Madrepora phrygia Ellis and Solander.

--- SENTENCE 1105 ---
Leptoria phrygia (Ell. and Sol.)-gracilis Dana.

--- SENTENCE 1106 ---
I have been considerably puzzled concerning these specimens and have been compelled to place them in this category until a larger suite can be available for study.

--- SENTENCE 1107 ---
Vaughan has discussed Leptoria phrygia and L. gracilis in his Murray Island work, pages 117 and 118.

--- SENTENCE 1108 ---
His plate 46, figure 1, shows Ellis and Solander's type of Madrepora phrygia, which is in the Hunterian Museum, Glasgow.

--- SENTENCE 1109 ---
Figures 4 and 4a of the same plate show Dana's type of Meandrina gracilis.

--- SENTENCE 1110 ---
The main differences in these types consist in the more crowded septa and the perhaps slightly sharper collines of the latter.

--- SENTENCE 1111 ---
Ellis and Solander's type contains "from 11 to 12 large septa to 1 cm.

--- SENTENCE 1112 ---
without or rarely with intermediate small septa, septal margins flat across the collines."

--- SENTENCE 1113 ---
Dana's type contains as many as 26 septa to the centimeter and "has broadly triangular collines, or the upper septal margins may be flatly arched.

--- SENTENCE 1114 ---
"

Plate 45, figure 5, and plate 46, figure 3, of Vaughan's paper show a specimen of L. phrygia from Cocos-Keeling Island which differs from the type by having more crowded septa and at times low, triangular septal margins over the collines.

--- SENTENCE 1115 ---
This shows a tendency towards L. gracilis.

--- SENTENCE 1116 ---
Plate 46, figures 4 and 4a, shows Dana's

specimen of L. gracilis from the Fiji Islands.

--- SENTENCE 1117 ---
The Samoan specimens illustrate an apparent intergradation between the species to a great extent.

--- SENTENCE 1118 ---
One specimen, especially, shows rather crowded septa whose upper margins are exceedingly flat.

--- SENTENCE 1119 ---
The number of septa per centimeter is intermediate between the two types.

--- SENTENCE 1120 ---
As no other criteria are known, it is very possible that the two species are variants of one.

--- SENTENCE 1121 ---
Until more specimens are available I am keeping them separate.

--- SENTENCE 1122 ---
The following is a brief description of specimen No. 2.

Corralum explanate, even-topped, undulating.

--- SENTENCE 1123 ---
Calices indistinct and meandering; system of meandering collines and valleys.

--- SENTENCE 1124 ---
Distance between colline summits is 3 to 4 mm.; depth of valleys 2.5 mm.

--- SENTENCE 1125 ---
Septa slightly exsert, cross walls, and generally fuse with corresponding septa of neighboring row; 18 or 19 in every centimeter.

--- SENTENCE 1126 ---
Septal margins nearly horizontal on top of collines, but at times form flat triangle.

--- SENTENCE 1127 ---
Septa drop nearly vertically into valleys; margins finely and regularly serrate.

--- SENTENCE 1128 ---
Septal faces spinulose with spinules occurring in rows so as to give septa a leaf-like appearance with venations.

--- SENTENCE 1129 ---
The columella is lamellar, with the spinulose, lamellate plates compressed parallel to the valleys.

--- SENTENCE 1130 ---
There are about 8, stretched out, one behind the other, in the valleys for every centimeter.

--- SENTENCE 1131 ---
Stations, Pago Pago Harbor, Tutuila, Samoa:

No. 1.

--- SENTENCE 1132 ---
Aua line, 700 to 724 feet from shore.

--- SENTENCE 1133 ---
No. 2 Shallow tide-pools on seaward edge of Utelei Reef.

#### Hydnophora microconos (Lamarck)-rigida (Dana.)

1918.

cit., p. 122, plate 47, figs. 3, 3a).

--- SENTENCE 1257 ---
Hydnophora rigida Vaughan (op.

--- SENTENCE 1259 ---
There is one specimen from the Utelei Reef which I am forced to place in this category, as it lies halfway between H. microconos and the specimen of H. rigida from Fanning Island which is pictured by plate 48, figure 2, in the above-mentioned work of Vaughan.

--- SENTENCE 1260 ---
The latter appears to be a rough-water specimen, whereas Dana's type of H. rigida is obviously a quiet-water one.

--- SENTENCE 1261 ---
The Samoan specimen was grown in shallow tide-pools on the seaward edge of the reef, and has the tendency to form short, thick branchlets.

--- SENTENCE 1262 ---
It has the growth-form of a rough-water variety of H. rigida, and yet the monticules and other features of the corallum are similar to H. microconos.

--- SENTENCE 1263 ---

#### Hydnophora mayori, new species.

--- SENTENCE 1265 ---
The lone specimen which Dr. Mayor collected in the Fiji Islands is a delicately branched example growing on lithothamnion.

--- SENTENCE 1266 ---
On the lower portions of the branches the monticules are distinct and placed in even longitudinal rows.

--- SENTENCE 1267 ---
Toward the ends they lose their individuality and become fused into ridges.

--- SENTENCE 1268 ---
The species is very similar to Dana's type of *Hydnophora rigida* (No. 148, U. S. Nat.

--- SENTENCE 1269 ---
Mus.), which was illustrated by Vaughan (Carnegie Inst.

--- SENTENCE 1270 ---
Wash. Pub. 213, plate 48, fig. 3).

--- SENTENCE 1271 ---
It is, however, much more delicate, with smaller corallum and thinner and shorter branches.

--- SENTENCE 1272 ---
If one of the small branchlets of Dana's type were

replaced by a branch of this species it would be difficult to note the change with the naked eye.

--- SENTENCE 1273 ---
Close observation, however, shows the septal edges of *H. mayori* to have distinct serrations of a sharp, toothlike character, increasing noticeably in size toward the valleys.

--- SENTENCE 1274 ---
This is lacking in *H. rigida*, which has nearly entire septal margins.

--- SENTENCE 1275 ---
The valleys of *H. mayori* are made rough by the serrations of the septal edges, which at times fuse to form a loose, irregular reticulum.

--- SENTENCE 1276 ---
Another difference between the two is that in this species the monticules are inclined toward the ends of the branches, while in *H. rigida*

--- SENTENCE 1277 ---
they generally stand out perpendicularly.

--- SENTENCE 1278 ---
The septal faces are finely granulose.

--- SENTENCE 1279 ---
There are a number of specimens in the U. S. National Museum, collected by J. B. Steere from the southern Philippines, which are very similar to *H. mayori*.

--- SENTENCE 1280 ---
I hesitate, however, to place them here because there is so little tendency to form individual monticules and because the serrations of the septal edges are very

unpronounced.

--- SENTENCE 1281 ---
Station, Suva Harbor, Fiji Islands.—No.

--- SENTENCE 1282 ---
Tide-pool on the barrier reef.

Distribution.—Fiji Islands; ?

--- SENTENCE 1283 ---
Philippine Islands.

--- SENTENCE 1313 ---
Wash. Pub. 213, p. 123, plate 49, figs.

--- SENTENCE 1314 ---
1, 2, 3; plate 50, figs.

--- SENTENCE 1315 ---
(With synonymy.)

Vaughan (op.

--- SENTENCE 1316 ---
cit., p. 123) includes in the synonymy of this species, Madrepora angulosa  $\gamma$  Ellis and Solander, Mussa costata Dana, Mussa sinuosa Dana, Mussa cytherea Dana, Mussa sinuosa Milne Edwards and Haime, Mussa brueggemanni Quelch, and Mussa brueggemanni Bedot.

--- SENTENCE 1317 ---
It is quite evident that there is a gradation between these forms.

--- SENTENCE 1318 ---
The variation within the species, however, is rather large, and it is advisable to keep in mind the direction in which it occurs.

--- SENTENCE 1319 ---
The three distinctive forms of the species are represented by the three names: (1) Mussa cytherea Dana, (2) Mussa sinuosa (Lamarck) = Mussa costata Dana, (3) Mussa brueggemanni Quelch.

--- SENTENCE 1320 ---
M. sinuosa (Lamarck) = M. costata Dana occupies the central position.

--- SENTENCE 1321 ---
All have the same septal dentation and differ mainly only in growth-form.

--- SENTENCE 1322 ---
the series are elongated, the branches compressed, the valleys narrow in proportion to their lengths, and the space between the series narrow.

--- SENTENCE 1323 ---
The septa drop at a steep angle into the valleys.

--- SENTENCE 1324 ---
In typical M. sinuosa (Lamarck) the series are not so elongated, the valleys wider in proportion to their lengths, the space between the series wider, and the septal margins grade at a gentler angle into the valleys.

--- SENTENCE 1325 ---
M. brueggemanni Quelch represents a still more advanced stage in this line of variation.

--- SENTENCE 1326 ---
The whole series may be likened to the gradual opening or closing of the petals of a flower.

--- SENTENCE 1327 ---
In M. brueggemanni the walls in places lie almost horizontal, with the lateral septal margins facing upward.

--- SENTENCE 1328 ---
The space between the calicular series is also increased.

--- SENTENCE 1329 ---
The Samoan collection contains 5 small branches from what apparently were 5 good-sized specimens.

--- SENTENCE 1330 ---
No. 1 is near the cytherea end of the series, No. 2 is typical sinuosa, and Nos.

--- SENTENCE 1331 ---
3, 4, and 5, are near the brueggemanni end.

--- SENTENCE 1332 ---
Stations, Pago Pago Harbor, Tutuila, Samoa:

Nos.

--- SENTENCE 1333 ---
1, 3, 4, 5.

--- SENTENCE 1334 ---
Pure agitated water in shallow tide-pools near seaward edge of Aua Reef.

--- SENTENCE 1335 ---
Aua Reef flat, 680 feet from shore, fairly agitated water, rocky bottom, I foot deep at low tide.

--- SENTENCE 1336 ---
Distribution.—Red Sea; Murray Island; Amboina; southern Philippines; Fiji Islands; Tahiti; Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 1355 ---

--- SENTENCE 1356 ---
Deep furrow in dividing-wall of series.

--- SENTENCE 1357 ---

--- SENTENCE 1358 ---

--- SENTENCE 1359 ---
in thickness in the wall, upper edges possessed with 2 or 3 very prominent teeth (2 to 3 mm.

--- SENTENCE 1360 ---
long); inner septal margins have 2 or 3 large teeth at the distal end and several smaller, more regular ones below.

--- SENTENCE 1361 ---

--- SENTENCE 1362 ---

#### Fungia paumotensis Stutchbury.

--- SENTENCE 1415 ---
See Dr. Mayor's "Growth-Rate of Samoan Corals," plate 25, fig.

--- SENTENCE 1416 ---
Fungia paumotensis Stutchbury, Trans.

--- SENTENCE 1417 ---
London, xvi, p. 485; plate 32, fig.

--- SENTENCE 1418 ---
Fungia paumotensis Döderlein, Korallengat.

--- SENTENCE 1419 ---
Fungia, p. 88, plate 7, figs.

--- SENTENCE 1420 ---
1 to 5. 1907.

--- SENTENCE 1421 ---
Fungia paumotensis Vaughan, U. S. Nat.

--- SENTENCE 1422 ---
59, p. 134, plate 35.

--- SENTENCE 1423 ---
I am referring to this species one specimen which was collected by Dr. Mayor in the Fiji Islands.

--- SENTENCE 1424 ---
It was broken in half and new parts had begun growing over the fractured sides.

--- SENTENCE 1425 ---
Combining both halves, its dimensions are as follows: length 123 mm., width 71 mm., thickness 21 mm., weight 8 ounces.

--- SENTENCE 1426 ---
One half inclined to arch above, the other half rather flat.

--- SENTENCE 1427 ---
Wall perforate near the margin, but solid in the large central area.

--- SENTENCE 1428 ---
Costæ crowded and fairly distinct to the central area.

--- SENTENCE 1429 ---
Ribs covered with small, bunchy, rough, close-fitting spines; 16 to 20 of latter in 1 cm.

--- SENTENCE 1430 ---
The roughness of the spines, their crowded and sometimes fused-together condition give the ribs a straggly appearance as they spoke out from the central area.

--- SENTENCE 1431 ---
Oral slit 40 by 4 mm.

--- SENTENCE 1432 ---
Septa unequal in height; those of the higher cycles much thinner than those of the lower, which are from 0.5 to 1 mm. thick.

--- SENTENCE 1433 ---
Large septa without perforations; small ones generally perforate.

--- SENTENCE 1434 ---
Margins with close-setting, fine teeth; faces covered with sharp, conical granulations.

--- SENTENCE 1435 ---
Septa crowded, 14 in 1 cm.

--- SENTENCE 1436 ---
at edge of corallum.

--- SENTENCE 1437 ---
Columella only fairly well developed and papillate in character.

--- SENTENCE 1438 ---
Distinctive characteristics.—This species is quite similar to F. scutaria Lamarck, but can be told from it by the fact that the septa are of equal height at the margins in the latter species and unequal in this species.

--- SENTENCE 1439 ---
Again, F. scutaria nearly always has well-developed tentacular lobes, whereas those of F. paumotensis are very insignificant, if present at all.

--- SENTENCE 1440 ---
The one exception to this is the typical Fungia scutaria Lamarck, which, according to Döderlein (op.

--- SENTENCE 1441 ---
cit., p. 95), possesses an indistinct or weak tentacular lobe.

--- SENTENCE 1442 ---
One small specimen of Fungia which has evidently recently been released from the attached anthocyathus stage was collected from the Utelei Reef, Pago Pago Harbor, Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 1443 ---
It is not sufficiently developed to make identification reliable,

although it undoubtedly belongs either to F. paumotensis or F. scutaria.

--- SENTENCE 1444 ---
The corallum is oval, size 56 by 40 by 11 mm.

--- SENTENCE 1445 ---
Oral slit 15 by 3 mm.

--- SENTENCE 1446 ---
Septal margins finely dentate in places and elsewhere smooth.

--- SENTENCE 1447 ---
Costæ distinct, possessed with rough, crowded spines which are often fused into short, straggly ridges.

--- SENTENCE 1448 ---
All septa project same distance at margin.

--- SENTENCE 1449 ---
Tentacular lobes very indistinct, but indications are present for development in this respect if specimen had continued growth.

--- SENTENCE 1450 ---
Papillate columella fairly well developed.

--- SENTENCE 1451 ---
The specimen probably belongs to F. scutaria Lamarck.

--- SENTENCE 1452 ---
Station.—Fiji Islands, mouth of Suva Harbor, on the barrier reef.

--- SENTENCE 1453 ---
The small, undeveloped specimen came from a tide-pool on seaward edge of

Utelei Reef, Pago Pago Harbor, Tutuila, Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 1454 ---
Distribution.—Paumotus; Tahiti; Philippine Islands (Quelch); Hawaiian Islands (Quelch); Amboina; Moluccas; Fiji Islands; ?

--- SENTENCE 1455 ---
Samoa.

#### Pachyseris speciosa (Dana).

3, 3a, 4, 4a.

--- SENTENCE 1479 ---
Pachyseris speciosa van der Horst, Mad.

--- SENTENCE 1480 ---
Siboga Exped., part 2, p. 35.

--- SENTENCE 1481 ---
Identification is based on Dana's type No. 199 and on a specimen from Murray Island described in the above-mentioned paper of Vaughan, both of which are in the U. S. National Museum.

--- SENTENCE 1482 ---
I refer 8 specimens to this species.

--- SENTENCE 1483 ---
They all show some slight variations, but in major characteristics are identical.

--- SENTENCE 1484 ---
All are fragments, with the exception of No. 1, which is a good-sized saucer-shaped specimen with the largest diameter measuring 24 cm.

--- SENTENCE 1485 ---
and the smallest 18 cm.

--- SENTENCE 1486 ---
On the underside there is an ample

attachment-stalk roughly 5 cm.

--- SENTENCE 1487 ---
The ridges are distinct in all parts of the colony, but increase in height and sharpness towards the margins of the corallum.

--- SENTENCE 1488 ---
The septa are identical with those of Dana's type No. 199.

--- SENTENCE 1489 ---
As in the latter, they are practically equal in size.

--- SENTENCE 1490 ---
On close observation, however, one can occasionally observe a row of darker colored, slightly thicker septa alternating with thinner, more projecting ones, which have their edges more crinkled.

--- SENTENCE 1491 ---
This feature is so insignificant that it can be recognized only on very close examination.

--- SENTENCE 1492 ---
All the septa have crinkled edges.

--- SENTENCE 1493 ---
The columellæ are composed of septa fused across the valleys and thus form an interrupted floor along the valley axes.

--- SENTENCE 1494 ---
I agree with van der Horst in placing P. haimei Quelch with this species.

--- SENTENCE 1495 ---
The differences as outlined by Quelch are so variable that they can not be of specific

value.

#### Pachyseris levicollis (Dana).

1846.

--- SENTENCE 1505 ---
Agaricia levicollis Dana, U. S. Expl.

--- SENTENCE 1506 ---
Exped., Zooph., p. 338, plate 22, fig.

--- SENTENCE 1507 ---
Pachyseris levicollis van der Horst, Mad.

--- SENTENCE 1508 ---
Siboga Exped., part 2, p. 36.

1922.

--- SENTENCE 1509 ---
Pachyseris levicollis van der Horst, Trans.

--- SENTENCE 1510 ---
London, 2d ser., Zool., vol. 18, p. 427.

--- SENTENCE 1511 ---
I have examined very closely, with the aid of the microscope, Dana's types of Agaricia levicollis and Agaricia speciosa, Nos.

--- SENTENCE 1512 ---
190 and 199 respectively of the U. S. National Museum.

--- SENTENCE 1513 ---
I find the following features of comparison:

(1) The ridges of P. speciosa are much higher than those of P. levicollis, which at times are nearly perfectly flat.

--- SENTENCE 1514 ---
Around the border of the corallum of the latter

they may be slightly elevated.

(2) The septa in P. levicollis are so nearly equal in size that it is impossible to see any alternation.

--- SENTENCE 1515 ---
Occasionally in the other species one can distinguish alternating septa on very close observation.

--- SENTENCE 1516 ---
(3) The septal edges of P. speciosa are more crinkled than those of the other

species, which at times may be nearly straight.

--- SENTENCE 1517 ---
(4) The columella of both species is essentially alike, although that of P. levi-

collis is perhaps a little better developed.

--- SENTENCE 1518 ---
Dr. Mayor collected one large specimen of this species and a small fragment.

--- SENTENCE 1519 ---
The large one was dredged dead and a good deal of it was lost.

--- SENTENCE 1520 ---
It is a big, explanate, undulating disk with the margins broken off.

--- SENTENCE 1521 ---
It measures 25 cm.

--- SENTENCE 1522 ---
by 22 cm., and towards the central area is 8 mm. thick.

--- SENTENCE 1523 ---
It offers a difference to the type in that the septal margins are decidedly crinkled.

--- SENTENCE 1524 ---
The ridges vary in width from 2 mm.

--- SENTENCE 1525 ---
Dana speaks of the ridges being narrow, and yet some in his type are 4 mm. broad.

--- SENTENCE 1526 ---
In general, those of the Samoan specimens are broader than those of the type.

--- SENTENCE 1527 ---
Due to the dead condition of the corallum, the columella does not show up well, although it seems to have been of the same degree of development as that of Dana's specimen.

--- SENTENCE 1528 ---
Stations, Pago Pago Harbor, Tutuila, Samoa:

No. 1.

--- SENTENCE 1529 ---
Dredged from depth of 108 feet, north shore, Aua Reef.

--- SENTENCE 1530 ---

#### Pachyseris carinata Brueggemann.

1879.

--- SENTENCE 1531 ---
Pachyseris carinata Brueggemann, Jour.

--- SENTENCE 1532 ---
Mus. Godeffroy, H. 14, p. 208.

1918.

--- SENTENCE 1533 ---
Pachyseris torresiana Vaughan, Carnegie Inst.

--- SENTENCE 1534 ---
Wash. Pub. 213, p. 132, plate 55, figs.

--- SENTENCE 1535 ---
1, 1a.

1921. ?

--- SENTENCE 1536 ---
Pachyseris rugosa van der Horst, Mad.

--- SENTENCE 1537 ---
Siboga Exped., part 2, p. 37, plate 4, fig.

--- SENTENCE 1538 ---
3; plate 6, fig.

--- SENTENCE 1539 ---
Pachyseris carinata van der Horst, Mad.

--- SENTENCE 1540 ---
Siboga Exped., part 2, p. 37, plate 5, fig.

--- SENTENCE 1541 ---
In van der Horst's paper mentioned above he believes that he recognizes among other species of *Pachyseris*, *P. rugosa*

--- SENTENCE 1542 ---
Lamarck, *P. carinata* Brueggemann, and *P. torresiana*

--- SENTENCE 1543 ---
Vaughan, all of which have, according to him, large bifacial fronds.

--- SENTENCE 1544 ---
The specimens which he has referred to these species resemble each other so closely that he thinks it very likely they all belong to the same species.

--- SENTENCE 1545 ---
From the remarks he makes concerning them and from his illustrations it certainly does appear that his surmise is correct.

--- SENTENCE 1546 ---
I am inclined to believe, however, that the specimens which he referred to *P. rugosa* do not belong there, but should be placed with *P. carinata*.

--- SENTENCE 1547 ---
As van der Horst himself has emphasized (op.

--- SENTENCE 1548 ---
cit., p. 37), according to Milne Edwards and Haime the septa of *P. rugosa* are distinctly alternating.

--- SENTENCE 1549 ---
This is not at all obvious in van der Horst's illustration (plate 4, fig. 3, 1921) of *P. rugosa*, neither is it characteristic of *P. carinata* and *P. torresiana*, to which he likens his *rugosa*.

--- SENTENCE 1550 ---
Also, I believe that *P. rugosa* Lamarck is not inclined to have large bifacial fronds, as is characteristic of the other two species, but rather a thin, irregular, undulating, explanate corallum.

--- SENTENCE 1551 ---
According to Milne Edwards and Haime, *P. rugosa* has very thin septa whereas in *P. carinata* and *P. torresiana*

--- SENTENCE 1552 ---
they are rather thick.

--- SENTENCE 1553 ---
I have before me Vaughan's types of *P. torresiana* and have no hesitancy in placing it with *P. carinata*.

--- SENTENCE 1554 ---
I do not believe that the shape of the fronds should

constitute a specific difference.

--- SENTENCE 1555 ---
From the points I have mentioned above I have come to the conclusion that *P. rugosa* (Lamarck) should probably be kept apart from *P. carinata*, and that in all likelihood van der Horst's *rugosa* belongs to *carinata*.

--- SENTENCE 1556 ---
The Samoan collection contains two splendid examples of this species.

--- SENTENCE 1557 ---
No. 1 is a colony of upright fronds from an irregular base.

--- SENTENCE 1558 ---
The manner of arrangement of the fronds resembles that of *Pavona decussata* somewhat.

--- SENTENCE 1559 ---

#### Pavona divaricata Lamarck.

--- SENTENCE 1576 ---
Also illustrated in Dr. Mayor's "Growth-Rate of Samoan Corals," plate 18, figs. 15d, 15s.

1816.

--- SENTENCE 1577 ---
Pavonia divaricata Lamarck, Anim. sans Vert., vol. 2, p. 240, No. 5.

1846.

--- SENTENCE 1578 ---
Pavonia divaricata Dana, U. S. Expl.

--- SENTENCE 1579 ---
Exped., Zooph., p. 327, plate 22, fig.

--- SENTENCE 1580 ---
Pavona divaricata van der Horst, Mad.

--- SENTENCE 1581 ---
Siboga Exped., pt. 2, p. 24.

1922.

--- SENTENCE 1582 ---
Pavona divaricata van der Horst, Trans.

--- SENTENCE 1583 ---
London, 2d ser., Zool., vol. 18, No. 6, p. 419.

--- SENTENCE 1584 ---
One of the most remarkable examples of the wide variation in the genus *Pavona* is afforded by the species *P. divaricata* Lamarck and *P. frondifera* Lamarck.

--- SENTENCE 1585 ---
Dana's original specimens of these two species are Nos.

--- SENTENCE 1586 ---
151 and 191, respectively, in the U. S. National Museum.

--- SENTENCE 1587 ---
The former has narrow, thick branches, very angular, keeled, and coalescent.

--- SENTENCE 1588 ---
The latter is made of broad plates with many carinæ and perpendicular platelets, so as to produce the subrectangular growth so characteristic of typical *P. decussata*.

--- SENTENCE 1589 ---
Two such apparently different specimens would certainly be widely separated specifically if intermediate forms were not available to prove their relationship.

--- SENTENCE 1590 ---
Verrill (Proc. Essex Inst., vol. 5, p. 44, 1867) described a new species, *P. foliosa*, the type of which is No. 413 U. S. National Museum.

--- SENTENCE 1591 ---
This is without question a young specimen of *P. frondifera* with thin crispate plates.

--- SENTENCE 1592 ---
There is an excellent suite of specimens in the museum collected by J. B. Steere in the southern Philippines, which prove this beyond a doubt.

--- SENTENCE 1593 ---
The relationship between the two is very similar to that existing between Dana's *P. boletiformis* and older specimens.

--- SENTENCE 1594 ---
Dr. Mayor collected a large number of specimens which bring out some very interesting facts.

--- SENTENCE 1595 ---
It is quite evident from a study of them and all other similar ones in the U. S. National Museum that the two species *P. divaricata* and *P. frondifera* grade towards each other to such an extent that it is nearly impossible to draw a dividing-line between them.

--- SENTENCE 1596 ---
Using as a starting-point the young specimen of Verrill's type of P. foliosa,

gradation seems to proceed along two lines: (1) normal, (2) irregular.<sup>1</sup>

By normal gradation or growth is meant that sort in which the plates retain their general shape, become thicker and broader, and finally reach the mature stage as exemplified by Dana's specimen of *P. frondifera*.

<sup>&</sup>lt;sup>1</sup>

--- SENTENCE 1597 ---
For an explanation concerning the illustrations showing these lines of variation see under *P. frondifera* Lamarck.

--- SENTENCE 1598 ---
By irregular gradation is meant that in which the plates become much curled at their growing tips, become keeled, and thus divide into delicate, narrow, angular branchlets.

--- SENTENCE 1599 ---
It is this latter direction of gradation which runs so near to *P. divaricata*.

--- SENTENCE 1600 ---
The two species may belong together, but I am keeping them separate on the follow-

ing grounds:

The fronds of *P. frondifera* are more delicate and thinner than *P. divaricata*.

--- SENTENCE 1601 ---
Those of the latter are thick, sturdy, and narrow.

--- SENTENCE 1602 ---
Although the tips may be sharp, the keels become thick within 1 or 2 mm. from the top.

--- SENTENCE 1603 ---
The fronds of the former taper slowly and delicately to the summits.

--- SENTENCE 1604 ---
Although both species have convex ambulacra, there is a tendency for this characteristic to be more pronounced in *P. frondifera*.

--- SENTENCE 1605 ---
In many places on the

specimens belonging to the other species the ambulacra are flat.

--- SENTENCE 1606 ---
The growth experiment described below under specimens 9a and 9b give further reason for keeping the species separate.

--- SENTENCE 1607 ---
I am referring the following 15 specimens to P. divaricata:

Stations, Pago Pago Harbor, Tutuila, Samoa:

Nos.

--- SENTENCE 1608 ---
Aua line, 766 to 790 feet from shore.

--- SENTENCE 1609 ---
These 3 specimens are nearly typical.

--- SENTENCE 1610 ---
The ambulacra are convex on basal portions and often flattened near tops of fronds.

--- SENTENCE 1611 ---
Columella absent or poorly developed; compressed style.

--- SENTENCE 1612 ---
Aua line, 700 to 724 feet from shore.

--- SENTENCE 1613 ---
No. 5

shows synapticulæ well; calices relatively thin.

--- SENTENCE 1614 ---
Aua line, 600 to 624 feet from shore.

--- SENTENCE 1615 ---
Fronds rather thin, approaching *P. frondifera*.

No. 7.

--- SENTENCE 1616 ---
Aua line, 526 to 550 feet from shore.

--- SENTENCE 1617 ---
Irregular specimen; columella fairly well developed.

--- SENTENCE 1618 ---
No. 8. 150 feet off mouth of big brook.

--- SENTENCE 1619 ---
Killed by fresh water and silt.

Nos.

--- SENTENCE 1620 ---
9a and 9b = Nos.

--- SENTENCE 1621 ---
15D and 15S (Mayor).

--- SENTENCE 1622 ---
These represent a growth experiment and are two halves of the same colony placed in different environments.

--- SENTENCE 1623 ---
No. 9a, which is exactly similar to Dana's specimen, was taken from relatively quiet, shallow water on the Aua Reef flat and placed in quiet water at a depth of 51 feet.

--- SENTENCE 1624 ---
No. 9b was placed on the breaker-washed edge of the Aua Reef patch.

--- SENTENCE 1625 ---
The latter half would hardly be recognized as belonging to the same species.

--- SENTENCE 1626 ---
The fronds have fused together completely and the corallum has taken on a dense, massive, convoluted shape until it resembles P. varians Verrill.

--- SENTENCE 1627 ---
This experiment has helped to strengthen my belief that the two species P. frondifera and P. divaricata should be kept separate, even on the apparently rather superficial criterion of the thickness of the keeled fronds.

--- SENTENCE 1628 ---
It shows that P. divaricata is not a rough-water facies of P. frondifera with correspondingly thicker fronds.

--- SENTENCE 1629 ---
No. 9a was taken from quiet water and placed in quiet water and still has the characteristic thick branches of typical P. divaricata.

--- SENTENCE 1630 ---
Shallow tide-pools on seaward edge of Utelei Reef.

--- SENTENCE 1631 ---
Rough-water specimen

similar to 9b.

--- SENTENCE 1632 ---
Nos. 11, 12, 13, and 14.

--- SENTENCE 1633 ---
Shallow tide-pools at low tide in agitated pure water near seaward edge of Aua Reef.

--- SENTENCE 1634 ---
No. 11 is a large, beautiful specimen.

--- SENTENCE 1635 ---
One side of it is identical with No. 9b, i. e., the branches have fused tightly to form a massive convoluted growth.

--- SENTENCE 1636 ---
As you pass from this edge towards the center the fronds gradually unfold themselves and separate from each other.

--- SENTENCE 1637 ---
When the other side is reached they have assumed the typical, keeled, divaricate structure, although somewhat dwarfed.

--- SENTENCE 1638 ---
No. 15, Aua line, 460 to 484 feet from shore.

--- SENTENCE 1639 ---
Heavy corallum, thick coalescent fronds, arched ambulacra, alternating septo-costæ, poorly developed styli-

form columella.

--- SENTENCE 1640 ---
Distribution.—Indian Ocean; Fiji Islands;

--- SENTENCE 1641 ---
Tongatabu; island of Ponapé; Indo-Australian Archipelago; Singapore; Wakaya; Rotuma; Samoa.

#### Pavona decussata Dana.

--- SENTENCE 1665 ---
Also Mayor's "Growth-Rate of Samoan Corals," plate 8, figs. 23, a, b.

- 1846.

--- SENTENCE 1666 ---
Pavonia decussata Dana, U. S. Expl.

--- SENTENCE 1667 ---
Exped., Zooph., p. 329, plate 22, fig.

--- SENTENCE 1668 ---
Pavonia boletiformis Dana, U. S. Expl.

--- SENTENCE 1669 ---
Exped., Zooph., p. 327, plate 22, fig. 7

--- SENTENCE 1670 ---
(non Lamarck).
- 1918.

--- SENTENCE 1671 ---
Pavona decussata Vaughan, Carnegie Inst.

--- SENTENCE 1672 ---
Wash. Pub. 213, p. 134.
  1918.

--- SENTENCE 1673 ---
Pavona danai Vaughan, Carnegie Inst.

--- SENTENCE 1674 ---
Wash. Pub. 213, p. 136, plate 55, fig.

--- SENTENCE 1675 ---
2; plate 56, figs.

--- SENTENCE 1676 ---
(With synonymy.)
- 1921.

--- SENTENCE 1677 ---
Pavona decussata van der Horst, Siboga Exped., part 2, p. 22.

--- SENTENCE 1678 ---
(With synonymy.)
- 1921.

--- SENTENCE 1679 ---
Pavona danai van der Horst, Siboga Exped., part 2, p. 23.
- 1922.

--- SENTENCE 1680 ---
Pavona decussata van der Horst, Trans.

--- SENTENCE 1681 ---
London, 2d ser., Zool., vol. 18, p. 418.

--- SENTENCE 1682 ---
Dana's original specimen of P. boletiformis (non Lamarck) type of Milne Edwards's Lophoseris danai is No. 136, U. S. National Museum.

--- SENTENCE 1683 ---
Vaughan has described and figured it (op.

--- SENTENCE 1684 ---
cit., p. 137, plate 56, figs.

--- SENTENCE 1685 ---
It is obviously a very young specimen, and because of this fact some difficulty has arisen regarding it and other closely related species.

--- SENTENCE 1686 ---
Dana's original specimens of P. decussata are also in the U. S. National Museum.

--- SENTENCE 1687 ---
The one which he described and figured has rather large fronds, which measure as much as 4 inches in breadth.

--- SENTENCE 1688 ---
The various specimens which have since been collected and described by later writers have served to bring closer together the type of P. danai and that of P. decussata, which by themselves appear quite different.

--- SENTENCE 1689 ---
Vaughan and van der Horst (op.

--- SENTENCE 1690 ---
cit., p. 22, 1921) agree that probably the principal difference between the two is the breadth of the fronds.

--- SENTENCE 1691 ---
However, both Vaughan's and van der Horst's specimens of Pavona danai have far broader fronds than Dana characterized as specific.

--- SENTENCE 1692 ---
Besides this, the two other original specimens of Dana's P. decussata (Nos. 167 and 200, U. S. National Museum) have much narrower fronds than those of his described specimen, and indeed often come within the limit of his P. boletiformis in this respect.

--- SENTENCE 1693 ---
It is quite evident, therefore, that

we can not depend on the lobation of the plates as the dividing-line between the two species.

--- SENTENCE 1694 ---
Both species have generally poorly developed carinæ, although those of P. decussata are usually thought of as being rather more pronounced.

--- SENTENCE 1695 ---
This character, however, can not be relied upon.

--- SENTENCE 1696 ---
The only differences that seem to be of distinguishing value are the following:

(1) The rows of calices are closer together in typical *P. decussata* and are more regular.

--- SENTENCE 1697 ---
The average distance between the row is 2.5 to 3 mm., whereas in *P. danai*

--- SENTENCE 1698 ---
it is 3.5 to 4 mm.

--- SENTENCE 1699 ---
In the latter they do not keep the same distance apart, but may be 2 mm.

--- SENTENCE 1700 ---
apart at one point and suddenly jump to 5 mm.

--- SENTENCE 1701 ---
within the distance of 3 septo-costæ.

(2) In P. decussata the rows of calices generally border closely the growing edge of the frond, whereas in P. danai there may be no calices within 10 mm. of the edge.

--- SENTENCE 1702 ---
(3) In P. danai the septo-costæ are lax and the larger ones stand out distinctly from the insignificant intermediates.

--- SENTENCE 1703 ---
In the other species they are relatively more

crowded and neater in appearance.

--- SENTENCE 1704 ---
The U. S. National Museum contains a large number of specimens which have been referred to one or the other of these species and which, together with those from Samoa, demonstrate a gradation between them.

--- SENTENCE 1705 ---
The specimen of *P. danai* from the Cocos-Keeling Islands which Vaughan described and figured (op.

--- SENTENCE 1706 ---
cit., p. 136, plate 55, fig.

--- SENTENCE 1707 ---
2) appears to represent the connecting-link between the two so-called species.

--- SENTENCE 1708 ---
The specimen from the Caroline Islands mentioned by Vaughan (op.

--- SENTENCE 1709 ---
cit., p. 137) and collected by Professor W. A. Bryan certainly bridges the gap between typical *P. danai* and the Cocos-Keeling specimen.

--- SENTENCE 1710 ---
The one from the Caroline Islands, however, has all the characteristics of typical *P. danai*, whereas the Cocos-Keeling one tends more towards *P. decussata*.

--- SENTENCE 1711 ---
The specimen from Zamboanga and the one from Mariveles, Luzon, which Vaughan mentions (op.

--- SENTENCE 1712 ---
cit., p. 137) also lie on the *P. decussata* side of the line.

--- SENTENCE 1713 ---
It is quite evident that there is a gradation from Dana's type of *P. danai*, through the Caroline Island specimen and similar ones from the Philippines in the U. S. National Museum, to those from Cocos-Keeling.

--- SENTENCE 1714 ---
Further gradation from these latter specimens through 4 collected by Dr. Mayor from the Samoa Islands and 2 from the Fiji Islands leads to typical *P. decussata*.

--- SENTENCE 1715 ---
The Cocos-Keeling specimen appears to represent the midway point with characteristics common to both

but inclining more towards P. decussata than P. danai.

--- SENTENCE 1716 ---
Since the gradation is so complete, I am combining the two under *P. decussata*.

--- SENTENCE 1717 ---
Those specimens which have the characteristics of *P. danai* as outlined above and which are on that side of the Cocos-Keeling specimen I consider belong to *P. decussata* var.

--- SENTENCE 1718 ---
Those on the other side belong to typical Pavona decussata Dana.

--- SENTENCE 1719 ---
It is possible that the variety danai simply represents the early stage in the growth of the corallum, as indeed many specimens seem to indicate.

--- SENTENCE 1720 ---
In this case it would of course not be a true variety.

--- SENTENCE 1721 ---
Since, however, there are some very well-developed specimens in the suite which have the characteristics of this variety, I feel justified in following the plan presented.

--- SENTENCE 1722 ---
I refer to typical *P. decussata* the four Samoan specimens and the two from the Fiji Islands.

--- SENTENCE 1723 ---
The latter (Nos. 4 and 5) are nearly identical with Dana's specimen No. 200, U. S. National Museum.

--- SENTENCE 1724 ---
The other four lie in between them

and the one from Cocos-Keeling described by Vaughan.

Stations:

Pago Pago Harbor, Tutuila, Samoa-

No. 1 = No. 23 (Mayor).

--- SENTENCE 1725 ---
From Aua Reef flat, 400 feet from shore, in quiet water about 8 inches deep at lowest tides.

--- SENTENCE 1726 ---
Aua line, 526 to 550 feet from shore.

--- SENTENCE 1727 ---
Aua line, 460 to 484 feet from shore.

--- SENTENCE 1728 ---
No. 6. 150 feet from mouth of big brook, killed by fresh water and silt.

--- SENTENCE 1729 ---

68F and 42F (Mayor).

Distribution.—Red Sea; Indian Ocean; Amboina; Sulu Sea; southern Philippines; Luzon, Philippines; Caroline Islands; Java; Sumatra; Billiton; Moluccas; Indo-Australian Archipelago; Fiji Islands; Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 1801 ---
