--- SENTENCE 1802 ---
It is a large colony of thick, erect, columnar fronds with rounded summits.

--- SENTENCE 1803 ---
The four Samoan specimens are insignificant compared to it in size.

--- SENTENCE 1804 ---
They are small, convex-topped, and possess no fronds.

--- SENTENCE 1805 ---
The calicular characters, however, are identical.

--- SENTENCE 1806 ---
Dana's figures of the type are very poor.

--- SENTENCE 1807 ---
Because of its bulk it can not be pictured here, but Nos.

--- SENTENCE 1808 ---
1 and 2 from Samoa illustrate the species very well.

--- SENTENCE 1809 ---
The following is a description of No. 1:

Small fragment from basal portion of corallum.

--- SENTENCE 1810 ---
Under surface free, completely covered by epitheca.

--- SENTENCE 1811 ---
At the rim it is only 2 mm. thick and gradually thickens up to 2 cm.

--- SENTENCE 1812 ---
at the place it is broken off.

--- SENTENCE 1813 ---
The calices are either completely surrounded by rounded collines or they may run in series of from 2 to 6 centers.

--- SENTENCE 1814 ---
The width of the collines is from 2.5 to 4 mm.; the average distance between the calices which lie in the same valley is 2 mm.

--- SENTENCE 1815 ---
The calicular fossæ are 0.5 mm.

--- SENTENCE 1816 ---
or less in diameter.

--- SENTENCE 1817 ---
The septa vary in number.

--- SENTENCE 1818 ---
In the individual calices, however, about 32 to 36 septa may be counted high up near the summit.

--- SENTENCE 1819 ---
Of these, 12 to 15 generally reach the calicular fossa; the rest fuse with them before the

center is reached.

--- SENTENCE 1820 ---
The septa are rather thick, usually thicker than the space between them.

--- SENTENCE 1821 ---
The septal edges are neatly covered with low, rough, blunt, subequal granules.

--- SENTENCE 1822 ---
The septal faces possess minute spines.

--- SENTENCE 1823 ---
In places the septo-costæ are continuous over the collines to those of the neighboring calice.

--- SENTENCE 1824 ---
In general, however, the summits of the collines become trabecular and take on a somewhat reticular appearance.

--- SENTENCE 1825 ---
The columella is fairly deep, distinct, and is made up of trabeculæ from the septal edges.

--- SENTENCE 1826 ---
3 and 4 differ from the other two by having slightly longer series and fewer individual calices.

--- SENTENCE 1827 ---
No. 4 is particularly distinguished in this respect and is also peculiar by having the collumellæ sunken lower than is usual.

--- SENTENCE 1828 ---
From the study of the specimens in regard to their environment it would appear that those grown in quiet water have more sloping collines and consequently

more open calices than those of rough-water habitat.

--- SENTENCE 1829 ---
As can be readily seen by the description and figures, this species is very similar to C. savigniensis (Gardiner).

--- SENTENCE 1830 ---
I doubt the validity of the latter.

--- SENTENCE 1831 ---
In describing the breadth of the collines, Gardiner must have measured only their summits, whereas I have recorded their entire width from fossa to fossa.

--- SENTENCE 1832 ---
The species is very similar to C. monile Forskål and appears to be, as Gardiner has remarked (Fauna and Geog.

--- SENTENCE 1833 ---
of Mal. and Lac.

--- SENTENCE 1834 ---
Arch., vol. 2, sup.

--- SENTENCE 1835 ---
concerning C. savigniensis, a denser edition of it.

--- SENTENCE 1836 ---
Dana's type of Psammocora fossata is No. 198, U. S. National Museum.

--- SENTENCE 1837 ---
This specimen certainly belongs to C. monile.

--- SENTENCE 1838 ---

#### Genus PSAMMOCORA Dana.

1846.

--- SENTENCE 1843 ---
Psammocora Dana, U. S. Expl.

--- SENTENCE 1844 ---
Exped., Zooph., p. 34.

--- SENTENCE 1845 ---
Type species: Pavonia obtusangula Lamarck.

--- SENTENCE 1846 ---
As van der Horst (1921 Siboga Exped., part 2, p. 32) pointed out, Stephanaria Verrill should be absorbed in Psammocora.

--- SENTENCE 1847 ---
Gardiner (Fauna and Geog. of the Mald. and Lac.

--- SENTENCE 1848 ---
Archipelagoes, vol. 2, supl. 1, part 3, p. 950) believes he can distinguish 4 separate species of the branching type of Psammocora: P. obtusangula (Lamarck), P. contigua (Esper), P. digitata Edw. and H., and P. divaricata Gardiner.

--- SENTENCE 1849 ---
It is quite a problem to know just what P. obtusangula is like.

--- SENTENCE 1850 ---
There is very little difference between it and P. contigua (Esper) in the descriptions of Milne Edwards and Haime.

--- SENTENCE 1851 ---
Gardiner sums up the differences by saying "P. contigua is distinguished by its less delimited and smaller calices and lesser number of septa."

--- SENTENCE 1852 ---
I have found that some specimens of P. contigua have rather large calices in places and the number of septa often is as much as that given by Edwards and Haime as characteristic of P. obtusangula (6 to 10).

--- SENTENCE 1853 ---
What is needed very badly is a good photograph of P. obtusangula.

--- SENTENCE 1854 ---
Gardiner believes P. contigua is the same as Dana's P. plicata (non Lamarck).

--- SENTENCE 1855 ---
Although Dana's original specimen of P. plicata is No. 217, U. S. National Museum, it is so badly corroded that it is impossible to express an opinion.

--- SENTENCE 1856 ---
Vaughan (1918, Carnegie Inst.

--- SENTENCE 1857 ---
Wash. Pub. 213, p. 141) believes it perhaps belongs to P. gonagra Klunzinger, which, according to Von Marenzeller (1906), is the same as P. planipora Ed. and H.

#### Psammocora contigua Esper.

--- SENTENCE 1858 ---
See also Mayor's "Growth-Rate of Samoan Corals."

1797.

--- SENTENCE 1859 ---
Madrepora contigua Esper, Die Pflanzentiere, p. 81, t. 66.

1886.

--- SENTENCE 1860 ---
Psammocora ramosa Quelch, Challenger Exp., Report on Reef-Corals, p. 128, plate 6, figs.

--- SENTENCE 1861 ---
Psammocora contigua Gardiner, Fauna and Geog. of Mald. and Laccad.

--- SENTENCE 1862 ---
Arch., vol. 2, sup. 1, p. 951.

--- SENTENCE 1863 ---
Psammocora contigua van der Horst, Siboga Exped, part 2, p. 33.

--- SENTENCE 1864 ---
(With synonymy.)

All of the branched specimens of *Psammocora* in the collection belong to this species.

--- SENTENCE 1865 ---
Gardiner recognizes two varieties, the typical and his variety *maldivensis*.

--- SENTENCE 1866 ---
I believe that both of these varieties are represented in the collection, but the only criterion on which I can divide them is the arrangement of the calices.

--- SENTENCE 1867 ---
In the type the calices are arranged in rows in the valleys on the branches with a good deal of coenenchyma between them.

--- SENTENCE 1868 ---
Variety *maldivensis* has the calices crowded together, with a corresponding lessening of coenenchyma and inconspicuous lines.

--- SENTENCE 1869 ---
Gardiner gives ample room for variation in growth-form.

--- SENTENCE 1870 ---
In this he was correct, as is demonstrated by the growth experiments outlined below.

--- SENTENCE 1871 ---
Some of the specimens have branches which are flattened out at their ends and at times are coalescent and twisted; others have crooked, rod-like branchlets, with irregular sides, generally about 15 mm.

--- SENTENCE 1872 ---
long and 4 mm. in diameter.

--- SENTENCE 1873 ---
Both types of branching occur on the same specimen, and it is merely a matter of which type pre-

dominates.

--- SENTENCE 1874 ---
I refer 6 specimens to the type form.

--- SENTENCE 1875 ---
The branches have on their sides longitudinal ridges which are devoid of calices.

--- SENTENCE 1876 ---
Between the ridges each valley is occupied by a line of calices.

--- SENTENCE 1877 ---
There are usually 4 thick, petal-like septa with distinct limits, between which are other septa which extend as a network beyond the calice and become lost in the coenenchyma.

--- SENTENCE 1878 ---
The coenenchyma occupies a good deal of space between the calice rows.

--- SENTENCE 1879 ---
The columella is rather inconspicuously situated below the inner edges of the septa.

--- SENTENCE 1880 ---
Five specimens belong to the variety maldivensis.

--- SENTENCE 1881 ---
They differ from the type only in the manner mentioned above.

--- SENTENCE 1882 ---
I fail to recognize any distinct differences

either in the number of septa or in the height of the columella.

#### Variety tutuilensis, new variety.

--- SENTENCE 1883 ---
One good-sized specimen shows such marked differences from the others that I

am placing it in a new variety.

--- SENTENCE 1884 ---
The growth is of the usual arborescent manner, with branches flattened.

--- SENTENCE 1885 ---
Average thickness of the latter, 4 mm.

--- SENTENCE 1886 ---
Near the top of the corallum they further branch, the branchlets being either delicately rod-shaped with rounded ends, or flattened, coalescent, and twisted, 5 to 20 mm. wide.

--- SENTENCE 1887 ---
Thin cylindrical rods often protrude from the sides of the branches.

--- SENTENCE 1888 ---
Except for these the sides are smooth, being practically free of ridges or humps.

--- SENTENCE 1889 ---
The calices are numerous, crowded together, and at times show indications of forming in rows.

--- SENTENCE 1890 ---
They are larger than those of the other varieties, the axial fossæ themselves being usually over 0.25 mm.

--- SENTENCE 1891 ---
The septa (6 to 10 in number) are of two kinds—one kind short, petal-like, of well-defined extent, 3 or 4 in number; the other, appearing between the former, of indefinite length, extending into the coenenchyma.

--- SENTENCE 1892 ---
The columella is usually distinct and consists in some cases of a single rounded rod pointed at the top, and in others has the shape of a cross.

--- SENTENCE 1893 ---
That the habitat has a great influence on the growth-form of *P. contigua* was adequately demonstrated by the growth experiments performed by Dr. Mayor.

--- SENTENCE 1894 ---
The usual experiment of dividing a colony in half and placing one part in deep.

quiet water and the other in shallow, agitated water was carried out on three examples.

--- SENTENCE 1895 ---
In all the cases the effect was similar.

--- SENTENCE 1896 ---
The parts grown in deep, quiet water have long, delicate, rod-like branches, twisted and with irregular sides.

--- SENTENCE 1897 ---
Those grown in rough water have shriveled up into well-nigh massive form, with branches fused and stunted.

--- SENTENCE 1898 ---
Besides the differences in growth-form, the quietwater halves are rougher in appearance, due to the fact that the septa and many of the protuberances of the coenenchyma stand out distinctly above the common level.

--- SENTENCE 1899 ---
Those specimens located in the ordinary habitat of the reef assumed the natural

form of growth of P. contigua.

--- SENTENCE 1900 ---

#### Psammocora samoensis, new species.

--- SENTENCE 1928 ---
This belongs to the group of massive Psammocoras.

--- SENTENCE 1929 ---
In growth-form it resembles very closely Psammocora nierstraszi van der Horst (Siboga Exped., part 2, p. 34, plate 2, figs.

--- SENTENCE 1930 ---
Dr. Mayor collected two examples which show a few differences.

--- SENTENCE 1931 ---
No. 1 is shaped roughly like a bent cylinder with rounded ends, and has no attachment scar.

--- SENTENCE 1932 ---
It measures about 8 cm.

--- SENTENCE 1933 ---
in length and 3 cm.

--- SENTENCE 1934 ---
Specimen No. 2 is a low, irregularly shaped mass with a large basal attachment scar.

--- SENTENCE 1935 ---
They are covered with irregular, twisted ridges of varying height.

--- SENTENCE 1936 ---
Reproduction takes place along the sides of the ridges near the top, and thus is produced in the rapidly growing portions of the corallum the arete-like structure so peculiar to this mode of propagation.

--- SENTENCE 1937 ---
In one place on No. 2 there are a few small cones such as van der Horst speaks of as characteristic of his P. nierstraszi.

--- SENTENCE 1938 ---
These are insignificant, however, and the entire remaining surface is made up of irregular,

twisted ridges of varying lengths and misshapen valleys.

--- SENTENCE 1939 ---
The calices are separately sunken in and the fossæ themselves average over 0.5 mm. in diameter.

--- SENTENCE 1940 ---
No. I varies from No. 2 in having thinner septa, although the number is common to both (12 to 13).

--- SENTENCE 1941 ---
The spaces between the septa in No. 1 are nearly as thick as the septa themselves.

--- SENTENCE 1942 ---
In No. 2 the latter are thicker and show very well the petal-shaped or elliptical septa and alternating rod-shaped ones, so characteristic of many Psammocoras.

--- SENTENCE 1943 ---
The elliptical septa are short, while the alternating ones extend farther up the ridge wall, where they fork, one prong becoming petal-shaped and the other straight.

--- SENTENCE 1944 ---
This pronging is arranged in such a way that in back of the first series of petal-shaped septa straight or rod-like ones extend; thus once again there is an alternating series of thick elliptical ones and straight ones.

--- SENTENCE 1945 ---
In their extension towards the top of the ridge, two more series of this kind may be seen at times.

--- SENTENCE 1946 ---
The limits of the series are marked by parallel rows of synapticulæ.

--- SENTENCE 1947 ---
This arrangement is best seen in the actively growing portions of the corallum.

--- SENTENCE 1948 ---
The elliptical rods are bulged and stand above the others, producing a rough surface.

--- SENTENCE 1949 ---
The septa are granulose.

--- SENTENCE 1950 ---
The columella is well developed.

--- SENTENCE 1951 ---
It generally consists of an irregular central hump with a variable number of smaller rods surrounding it.

--- SENTENCE 1952 ---
These are at times so tangled up and fused with one another that no definite structure can be made out.

--- SENTENCE 1953 ---
Stations.—Pago Pago Harbor, Tutuila, Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 1954 ---
1 and 2, 225 feet and 200 feet respectively inward from seaward edge of Aua Reef flat.

--- SENTENCE 1955 ---
Shallow, relatively agitated water; rocky bottom.

#### Astreopora profunda Verrill.

1846.

--- SENTENCE 2004 ---
Astræopora pulvinaria Dana (non Lamarck), Zooph., p. 415, plate 29, figs. 3, 3a, 3b, 3c.

--- SENTENCE 2005 ---
Astræopora profunda Verrill, appendix to Dana's Corals and Coral Islands, p. 333.

1896.

--- SENTENCE 2006 ---
Astraopora profunda Bernard, Cat.

--- SENTENCE 2007 ---
Mus., vol. 2, p. 98, plate 30; plate 33, fig.

--- SENTENCE 2008 ---
Identification based on one of the type specimens, No. 208, U. S. National Museum.

--- SENTENCE 2009 ---
Two specimens were collected from Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 2010 ---
I has a somewhat explanate growth-form, while No. 2 is subhemispherical

and contains slightly larger calices than the type and No. 1.

--- SENTENCE 2011 ---
No. 1 is a fragment of what was evidently a good-sized corallum.

--- SENTENCE 2012 ---
The upper surface is convex.

--- SENTENCE 2013 ---
Some of the calices have their margins elevated, while others are even with the surface of the coenenchyma.

--- SENTENCE 2014 ---
As a rule they are subcircular, but often they are distorted.

--- SENTENCE 2015 ---
The fully developed corallites measure from 1.5 to 2.5 mm. across their calicular openings.

--- SENTENCE 2016 ---
Small calices appear frequently in the depressions between the mature ones.

--- SENTENCE 2017 ---
Distance between the larger calices is from 1.5 to 3 mm., usually about 2 mm.

--- SENTENCE 2018 ---
Depth of calices 3 to 4 mm.

--- SENTENCE 2019 ---
There are usually two distinct cycles of septa, with the rudiments of a third frequently visible.

--- SENTENCE 2020 ---
The six primaries are much more prominent than the secondaries.

--- SENTENCE 2021 ---
The former are narrow near the margin and become so broad farther down that they fuse and form a fairly distinct columella.

--- SENTENCE 2022 ---
Occasionally one or two secondaries may reach the columella, but in general they are not nearly broad enough.

--- SENTENCE 2023 ---
In the type the edges of the septa possess moderate serrations.

--- SENTENCE 2024 ---
This is not so pronounced in this specimen.

--- SENTENCE 2025 ---
The coenenchyma is rather porous.

--- SENTENCE 2026 ---
The surface is possessed with tall, roughly terminated spines.

--- SENTENCE 2027 ---
The latter are best developed around the calicular margins.

--- SENTENCE 2028 ---
Very perforate and only fairly continuous platforms are formed by the coenenchyma.

--- SENTENCE 2029 ---
This species is very similar to A. myriopthalma (Lamarck), but can be distinguished mainly by the presence of a columella.

--- SENTENCE 2030 ---
In the latter species the septa do not fuse to make a distinct columella and a rudimentary tertiary is rarely seen.

--- SENTENCE 2031 ---
Stations, Pago Pago Harbor, Tutuila, Samoa:

No. 1.

--- SENTENCE 2032 ---
Shallow tide-pools on seaward edge of Utelei Reef.

--- SENTENCE 2033 ---
Pure agitated water in shallow tide-pools near outer edge of Aua Reef.

--- SENTENCE 2035 ---
#### Genus MONTIPORA Quoy and Gaimard.

--- SENTENCE 2036 ---
Montipora Quoy and Gaimard, Voy. de l'Astrolabe, Zool., vol.

--- SENTENCE 2037 ---
Cat. Madreporaria, vol. 3, pp. 1-166, 177-184.

--- SENTENCE 2038 ---
Type species: Montipora verrucosa Quoy and Gaimard (non Lamarck) =

Montipora foveolata (Dana).

--- SENTENCE 2039 ---
The genus *Montipora* is one of the most interesting of coral genera.

--- SENTENCE 2040 ---
Bernard, in his splendid work on the Montiporæ of the British Museum, has contributed a great deal to our knowledge of this genus.

--- SENTENCE 2041 ---
He makes the variations in the coenenchyma the basis for his five divisions.

--- SENTENCE 2042 ---
Following is his key to the divisions:

I. Glabrous, in which the surface is smooth.

II.

--- SENTENCE 2043 ---
Glabro-foveolate, in which the foveolate appearance is not very marked, or perhaps only confined to the younger portions of the stock, where growth is very rapid.

--- SENTENCE 2044 ---
Foveolate, in which the interstices swell up into continuous or interrupted ramparts.

--- SENTENCE 2045 ---
(a) In which the interstices swell up in irregular patches.

--- SENTENCE 2046 ---
(b) In which the papillæ form hoods or underlips, etc., to the calices.

--- SENTENCE 2047 ---
(c) In which the papillæ run together to form ridges.

--- SENTENCE 2048 ---
(d) In which the papillæ form regular rounded or nipple-shaped swellings.

--- SENTENCE 2049 ---
(a) In which the tubercles are distinct.

--- SENTENCE 2050 ---
(b) In which the tubercles run together to form ridges.

--- SENTENCE 2051 ---
Jahrb., Heft 5, 1901, p. 396) reported Montipora fungiformis Bernard and M. lobulata Bernard from Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 2052 ---
Neither of these species is represented in this collection.

--- SENTENCE 2053 ---
Jahrb., III, 1888, p. 155) described Montipora scabriculoides from Samoa, which he subsequently identified as Montipora exerta Quelch.

#### Montipora vaughani, new species.

--- SENTENCE 2055 ---

long and 6 cm.

--- SENTENCE 2057 ---

--- SENTENCE 2058 ---

where it is broken off.

--- SENTENCE 2060 ---
The main part of the corallum was probably considerably thicker.

--- SENTENCE 2061 ---
The surface is smooth and undulating.

--- SENTENCE 2062 ---
The border is rounded and the calices appear on the underside as far as 2 cm.

--- SENTENCE 2063 ---
from the edge, where they meet the epitheca.

--- SENTENCE 2064 ---
The interstitial elevations of the coenenchyma rise to the same level above the calices, and since they are fused together they form irregularly meandering ridges around the calices.

--- SENTENCE 2065 ---
The latter are conspicuous and deeply set (about 2 mm.) between the ridges, which have nearly vertical sides and drop immediately into the calicular fossa.

--- SENTENCE 2066 ---
The ridges may inclose but one calice or there may be as many as six strung out side by side in a row and jammed against each other in the short valleys.

--- SENTENCE 2067 ---
The calices average about 0.8 mm.

--- SENTENCE 2068 ---
in diameter; the distance across the valleys from one ridge to another is 0.8 to 1 mm.; the thickness of the ridges averages 1 mm.

--- SENTENCE 2069 ---
The septa are well developed.

--- SENTENCE 2070 ---
Six thick primaries nearly meet in the center of each calice; they can be seen to fuse a short distance below the calicular surface.

--- SENTENCE 2071 ---
Two directives can usually be made out by their slightly greater breadth and thickness.

--- SENTENCE 2072 ---
The six intermediates are thick, but usually short.

--- SENTENCE 2073 ---
The dense, streaming reticulum rises from just above the epitheca and shoots upward in the form of thin, closely placed rods joined together by transverse processes.

--- SENTENCE 2074 ---
The coenenchyma rises 2 mm. above the surface of the calices and ends as continuous, rounded-topped walls of the same height and 1 mm. thick.

--- SENTENCE 2075 ---
The entire coenenchyma is a rather dense reticulum, although when enlarged and viewed from the top it appears to be somewhat spongy.

--- SENTENCE 2076 ---
This distinctive and interesting species probably belongs to the foveolate group of Bernard.

--- SENTENCE 2077 ---
It could also be regarded as belonging to the papillate group,

with regularly fused papillæ.

--- SENTENCE 2078 ---
The specimen described is the only one in the collection, with the exception of a

smaller fragment from the same station.

--- SENTENCE 2079 ---
Station.—Pago Pago Harbor, Tutuila, Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 2080 ---
Shallow water, washed by breakers on seaward edge of Utelei Reef.

#### PAPILLATE MONTIPORÆ.

#### Montipora venosa (Ehrenberg).

--- SENTENCE 2081 ---
Porites venosa Ehrenberg, Korallenthiere, p. 118.

1897.

--- SENTENCE 2082 ---
Montipora venosa Bernard, Brit.

--- SENTENCE 2083 ---
Mus. Cat. Madreporaria, vol. 3, p. 69, plate 32, fig.

--- SENTENCE 2084 ---
Montipora venosa Vaughan, Carnegie Inst.

--- SENTENCE 2085 ---
Wash. Pub. 213, p. 153, plate 63, fig. 3; plate 19, fig.

--- SENTENCE 2086 ---
There are three specimens belonging to this species in the collection.

--- SENTENCE 2087 ---
They correspond with Bernard's description and illustration so well that it is unnecessary for me to describe them in detail.

--- SENTENCE 2088 ---
Two are incrusting, rather heavy, and from 1 to 2 cm.

--- SENTENCE 2089 ---
The third is a large, heavy, subspherical specimen with a maximum diameter of 12 and a minimum one of 10 cm.

--- SENTENCE 2090 ---
The upper surface is made of an irregularly scattered reticulum, occuring as single papillæ or as rings around the calices.

--- SENTENCE 2091 ---
Between the papillæ smooth valleys are scattered indiscriminately, so that the surface has a patchy appearance.

--- SENTENCE 2092 ---
The calices are from 0.5 to 0.75 mm. in diameter.

--- SENTENCE 2093 ---
Six well-developed primary

septa alternate with weakly developed secondaries.

--- SENTENCE 2094 ---
cit., p. 153) specimens in the U. S. National Museum from Murray Island, Australia, differ from these and the description of Bernard considerably.

--- SENTENCE 2095 ---
The calices are much larger and less crowded, the septa better developed, and the surface of the corallum much more even in the case of the former.

--- SENTENCE 2096 ---
It is evident, however, that they belong to this species, with perhaps one exception.

--- SENTENCE 2097 ---
The specimen from the lithothamnion ridge should very probably be separated.

--- SENTENCE 2098 ---
The latter is foveolate, has no papillæ, and the two cycles of septa are exceedingly well developed and remarkably regular.

--- SENTENCE 2099 ---
The smooth, regular appearance of the surface offers a marked contrast to the rough, irregular surface of typical M. venosa.

--- SENTENCE 2100 ---

#### Montipora tuberculosa (Lamarck).

--- SENTENCE 2105 ---
3a, 3b, 3c.

1816.

--- SENTENCE 2106 ---
Porites tuberculosa Lamarck (non Klunzinger), Anim. sans Vert., ii, p. 272. 1897.

--- SENTENCE 2107 ---
Montipora tuberculosa Bernard, Brit.

--- SENTENCE 2108 ---
Montipora, p. 112. (Synonymy.)

--- SENTENCE 2109 ---
I have hesitated somewhat between putting this specimen here or with Montipora sinensis Bernard.

--- SENTENCE 2110 ---
Bernard says concerning the two: "This type [M. sinensis] is probably allied with M. tuberculosa of Lamarck, from which, however, it differs mainly in the size of the calices, which are much smaller in Lamarck's type."

--- SENTENCE 2111 ---
The calices of M. sinensis measure 0.75 mm. in diameter, while in Lamarck's type they are only 0.4 mm.

--- SENTENCE 2112 ---
The Samoan specimen seems to lie between the two in this respect, with calices of 0.5 to 0.7 mm. and averaging 0.6 mm.

--- SENTENCE 2113 ---
I am placing it with M. tuberculosa because it corresponds to it better in the following minor differences:

- (1) M. sinensis has calices which are "sunk somewhat below the surface of the coenenchyma as indentations," which is not the case in this species.
  - (2) The calices are slightly more crowded in M. tuberculosa (1 mm. apart).
- (3) There is more of a tendency for the papillæ to unite to form crests in M. tuberculosa.
- (4) Bernard says concerning *M. sinensis:*

--- SENTENCE 2114 ---
To the naked eye the septa seem to fill up the cavity so that no dark fossa is visible."

--- SENTENCE 2115 ---
This impression is not given in the Samoan specimen.

--- SENTENCE 2116 ---
Bernard's illustration of *M. sinensis* (plate 19, fig. 3), however, seems to show dark fossæ rather plainly.

--- SENTENCE 2117 ---
Station.—Pago Pago Harbor, Tutuila, Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 2118 ---
Shallow tide-pools on seaward

edge of Utelei Reef.

Distribution.—Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 2119 ---
#### Montipora elschneri Vaughan.

1918.

--- SENTENCE 2120 ---
Montipora elschneri Vaughan, Carnegie Inst.

--- SENTENCE 2121 ---
Wash. Pub. 213, p. 154, plate 64, fig.

--- SENTENCE 2122 ---
Both specimens of this species in the collection are incrusting and have nearly completely surrounded the dead coral on which they grew.

--- SENTENCE 2123 ---
In this respect they differ from the type (in the U. S. National Museum), the corallum of which is an irregularly shaped, thick plate.

--- SENTENCE 2124 ---
The surface, the corallites, and the coenenchyma, however, are so similar that they must belong to this species.

--- SENTENCE 2125 ---
The upper surface is identical with that of the type, except that the low elevations or lumps are more pronounced.

--- SENTENCE 2126 ---
The undersurface is smooth and entirely without papillæ.

--- SENTENCE 2127 ---
On the under surface the calices are less distinct and slightly

smaller (0.4 to 0.5 mm.)

--- SENTENCE 2128 ---
than those above (0.5 to 0.7 mm.).

--- SENTENCE 2129 ---
As a rule, the tops of the calices are even with the coenenchymal surface.

--- SENTENCE 2130 ---
On specimen No. 2, however, the calicular openings have very frequently been carried upward by surrounding coenenchyma.

--- SENTENCE 2131 ---
This, of course, makes the surface much rougher.

--- SENTENCE 2132 ---
The six primary septa are well developed, but scarcely ever extend beyond the

half-diameter circle.

--- SENTENCE 2133 ---
The intermediate septa are distinct.

--- SENTENCE 2134 ---
This species seems to have some points in common with Von Marenzeller's

Montipora densa.

--- SENTENCE 2135 ---
Station.—Pago Pago Harbor, Tutuila, Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 2136 ---
1 and 2, 766 to 790 feet from shore, Aua line.

--- SENTENCE 2137 ---
Distribution.—Fanning Island; Samoa.

#### TUBERCULATE MONTIPORÆ.

#### Montipora ehrenbergii Verrill.

--- SENTENCE 2138 ---
Porites foliosa (partim) Ehrenberg (non Pallas), Korallenthiere, p. 117.

1875.

--- SENTENCE 2139 ---
Montipora ehrenbergii Verrill, in Dana's Corals and Coral Islands, p. 333.

1879.

--- SENTENCE 2140 ---
Montipora tuberosa Klunzinger, Korallenthiere, 11, p. 32, plate 6, fig. 6; plate 5, fig. 8; plate 10, fig.

--- SENTENCE 2141 ---
Montipora tuberosa Bernard, Brit.

--- SENTENCE 2142 ---
Montipora, vol. 3, p. 136.

--- SENTENCE 2143 ---
There are 4 specimens of this species in the Samoan collection.

--- SENTENCE 2144 ---
All are explanate, with turned-up margins wherever free.

--- SENTENCE 2145 ---
The surface rises into irregular knobs and lobes.

--- SENTENCE 2146 ---
1 and 2 are possibly pieces of the same colony.

--- SENTENCE 2147 ---
No. 1 measures 12 by 8.5 cm.;

--- SENTENCE 2148 ---
No. 2 measures 19 by 10 cm.

--- SENTENCE 2149 ---
The others are small fragments.

--- SENTENCE 2150 ---
Thus the Samoan specimens are much smaller than that described by Klunzinger.

--- SENTENCE 2151 ---
The knobs and lobes are not so high as those usually attributed to this species.

--- SENTENCE 2152 ---
The highest is 2.5 cm.; the thickest 2 cm.

--- SENTENCE 2153 ---
The tallest tubercles never exceed 1.5 mm., the majority being in the neighborhood of 1 mm.

--- SENTENCE 2154 ---
The tubercles are slightly taller and more dense on the knobs and at times fuse to form short, thin, irregular walls between the calices.

--- SENTENCE 2155 ---
This latter characteristic can often be observed on Dana's type of *Montipora hispida*, No. 341, U. S. National Museum, which is very similar in many respects.

--- SENTENCE 2156 ---
The explanate basal portions have upturned edges.

--- SENTENCE 2157 ---
This and the fact that the tubercles are not so reguarly distributed over the upper surface are the main fea-

tures which distinguish it from M. hispida (Dana).

--- SENTENCE 2158 ---
The free-growing young margins of the corallum are somewhat different from the interior and older portions.

--- SENTENCE 2159 ---
The calices are not so crowded and are frequently arranged in rows, slightly elevated, with valleys between.

--- SENTENCE 2160 ---
The calices appear as protuberant rings which rise with the rings of tubercles.

--- SENTENCE 2161 ---
I think it very probable that Bernard's *Montipora annularis* (op.

--- SENTENCE 2162 ---
cit., p. 115, plate 23, fig.

--- SENTENCE 2163 ---
4; plate 33, fig. 15) represents a very young form of *M. ehrenbergii*.

--- SENTENCE 2164 ---
Specimen No. 3 resembles it very much.

--- SENTENCE 2165 ---
There is a specimen from the Philippines collected by J. B. Steere, which is

typical M. ehrenbergii.

--- SENTENCE 2166 ---
Station.—Pago Pago Harbor, Tutuila, Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 2167 ---
Aua line, 766 to 790 feet from shore.

--- SENTENCE 2168 ---
Distribution.—Red Sea; Philippines; Samoa.

#### Montipora trabeculata Bernard.

--- SENTENCE 2169 ---
Montipora trabeculata Bernard, Brit.

--- SENTENCE 2170 ---
Montipora, p. 148, plate 27, fig. 2; plate 34, fig.

--- SENTENCE 2171 ---
Two medium-sized specimens and a fragment of this species were collected

by Dr. Mayor.

--- SENTENCE 2172 ---
The corallum consists of an irregular basal plate, which sends up compact tufts of vertical branches.

--- SENTENCE 2173 ---
The branches divide, the branchlets being subcylindrical, upright, and very crowded.

--- SENTENCE 2174 ---
They often fuse to form ridges.

--- SENTENCE 2175 ---
One outstanding feature is the fact that the branchlets are flat-topped and all end at practically the same level.

--- SENTENCE 2176 ---
Diameter of average branchlet is 3 to 4 mm.

--- SENTENCE 2177 ---
Specimen No. 2 does not have the tubercles as crowded as in No. 1.

--- SENTENCE 2178 ---
Bernard's description is adequate.

--- SENTENCE 2179 ---
Plate 7, figure 2, shows a natural-sized view of No. 2.

--- SENTENCE 2180 ---
It is possible that this species is a branching variety of the preceding.

Mus., p. 168, plate 63, figs.

--- SENTENCE 2186 ---
2, 2a, 2b; plate 64, figs. 1, 1a.

--- SENTENCE 2187 ---
Montipora verrilli Vaughan, Carnegie Inst.

--- SENTENCE 2189 ---
I am referring 3 specimens to typical M. verrilli and have been able to compare them to all those from Hawaii and the Fanning Islands in the U. S. National Museum.

#### Variety auaensis, new variety.

--- SENTENCE 2200 ---
One specimen shows such differences that I am making it the type of a new

variety.

--- SENTENCE 2201 ---
Following is a description of it:

--- SENTENCE 2202 ---
Very irregular surface covered with low, pimple-like mounds and ridges.

--- SENTENCE 2203 ---
Very thin where there is no free edge (2 mm.), but 11 mm. thick where it extends beyond lithothamnion which it incrusts.

--- SENTENCE 2204 ---
Upper surface has soft, woolly, fleecy appearance.

--- SENTENCE 2205 ---
Calices are very inconspicuous.

--- SENTENCE 2206 ---
Dark spots are not plainly visible where fossæ are present.

--- SENTENCE 2207 ---
This is due partly to the exceedingly well developed septa.

--- SENTENCE 2208 ---
The calices are rather numerous, though irregularly placed.

--- SENTENCE 2209 ---
In places they jam up close together and at others are widely separated.

--- SENTENCE 2210 ---
They are closer together on the center of the corallum and less numerous at the margins.

--- SENTENCE 2211 ---
They are minute, ranging from 0.4 mm.

--- SENTENCE 2212 ---
A few, however, which are raised by the tubercles measure 1 mm. in diameter.

--- SENTENCE 2213 ---
The calices of the underside average about 0.4 mm. in diameter.

--- SENTENCE 2214 ---
They are 1 mm.

--- SENTENCE 2215 ---
apart and are surrounded by a distinct reticular ring.

--- SENTENCE 2216 ---
Of the six stout primary septa the two directives generally fuse in the center of the calice and the others frequently extend to the point of fusion.

--- SENTENCE 2217 ---
This gives rise to a false columella.

--- SENTENCE 2218 ---
The intermediate secondary septa are also well developed and often reach to nearly the half-diameter circle.

--- SENTENCE 2219 ---
The calicular margins are distinct.

--- SENTENCE 2220 ---
I have seen a few of the larger calices having beginnings of a third cycle

of septa.

--- SENTENCE 2221 ---
The coenenchyma is made up of rather dense reticulum with very minute, rough granulations extending above the surface.

--- SENTENCE 2222 ---
Cross-section shows a thin, streaming layer with thin trabeculæ running to the upper and lower surfaces.

--- SENTENCE 2223 ---
Those bending upward rise above the surface in the form of tubercles.

--- SENTENCE 2224 ---
The junctions between the upward-streaming trabeculæ are slightly stouter than those below.

--- SENTENCE 2225 ---
The tubercles are very irregular in size and shape.

--- SENTENCE 2226 ---
They are generally bushy, and range in height from 0.4 to 1.5 mm.

--- SENTENCE 2227 ---
In some parts of the corallum they form rings of individual tubercles around the calices and at other parts fuse to form ridges which run irregularly in all directions between the calices.

--- SENTENCE 2228 ---
In places they

are very similar to the ridges of some papillate species, such as *Montipora prolifera* Brueggemann.

--- SENTENCE 2229 ---
The calices are found both in the valleys between the ridges and

also on the pimple-like mounds and ridges.

--- SENTENCE 2230 ---
This variety is distinguished from typical *Montipora verrilli* mainly by its very inconspicuous calices, well-developed septa and the fact that the tubercles fuse and run as ridges.

--- SENTENCE 2231 ---
Some places on the corallum appear as typical *M. verrilli*, except for the inconspicuous calices.

--- SENTENCE 2232 ---
At an ordinary distance from the eyes it is nearly impossible to distinguish any fossæ.

--- SENTENCE 2233 ---
Station.—Pago Pago Harbor, Tutuila, Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 2234 ---
Shallow tide-pools on seaward

edge of Utelei Reef, in breakers.

--- SENTENCE 2235 ---
Distribution of Montipora verrilli.—Hawaiian Islands; Fanning Islands; Samoa.

#### Montipora berryi, new species.

--- SENTENCE 2236 ---
Corallum is a small, solid, incrusting plate with an irregularly convex upper surface.

--- SENTENCE 2237 ---
It measures 8 by 5 cm.

--- SENTENCE 2238 ---
and is from 2 to 9 mm. thick.

--- SENTENCE 2239 ---
The calices are very crowded, less than their diameter apart, and frequently jammed together.

--- SENTENCE 2240 ---
They average 0.7 mm. across and are only fairly distinct amid the forest of tall tubercles.

--- SENTENCE 2241 ---
The septa are well developed, the six primaries often crossing beyond the half-diameter circle.

--- SENTENCE 2242 ---
The secondaries are unevenly although well developed, and some may be nearly as broad as the primaries.

--- SENTENCE 2243 ---
Two large directives usually are conspicuous.

--- SENTENCE 2244 ---
The primaries can frequently be seen to fuse lower down in the calice, a

styliform columella at times resulting.

--- SENTENCE 2245 ---
The surface of the corallum is very rough, because of numerous low humps and ridges closely crowded together, with corresponding narrow valleys between them.

--- SENTENCE 2246 ---
From the rather densely reticular streaming layer trabeculæ flow to the upper and lower surfaces.

--- SENTENCE 2247 ---
The trabeculæ are of different thicknesses, but on the whole are rather stout and are held together by transverse partitions.

--- SENTENCE 2248 ---
Those going upward rise high above the surface in the form of tall, fairly even, bushy tubercles which extend as much as 2 mm.

--- SENTENCE 2249 ---
above the tops of the calices.

--- SENTENCE 2250 ---
The entire space between the calices is taken up by these tubercles.

--- SENTENCE 2251 ---
Those on the raised portions of the corallum are larger than those in the valleys.

--- SENTENCE 2252 ---
They generally form rings around the calices (6 to 8 surrounding each calice).

--- SENTENCE 2253 ---
On the raised portions they frequently fuse so as to form a solid wall.

--- SENTENCE 2254 ---
These walls are not long, however, and are seldom made up of more than 2 or 3 fused tubercles.

--- SENTENCE 2255 ---
These large tubercles give the corallum an exceedingly rough appearance.

--- SENTENCE 2256 ---
This species is somewhat similar to M. effusa Dana, the type of which is No. 316, U. S. National Museum.

--- SENTENCE 2257 ---
It is separated from the latter by its growth-form, its closely packed calices, broader septa, slightly taller, more crowded tubercles,

and heavier corallum.

--- SENTENCE 2258 ---
Station.—Pago Pago Harbor, Tutuila, Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 2259 ---
Pure agitated water in shallow tide-pools near seaward edge of Aua Reef.

--- SENTENCE 2260 ---

#### Acropora formosa (Dana).

--- SENTENCE 2298 ---
Plate 8, fig. 1

--- SENTENCE 2299 ---
(Dana's type No. 888, U. S. Nat.

--- SENTENCE 2300 ---
3a, 3b.

- 1846.

--- SENTENCE 2301 ---
Madrepora formosa Dana, U. S. Expl.

--- SENTENCE 2302 ---
Exped., Zooph., p. 473, plate 31, fig.

--- SENTENCE 2303 ---
2; plate 38, fig.

--- SENTENCE 2304 ---
Madrepora brachiata Dana, U. S. Expl.

--- SENTENCE 2305 ---
Exped., Zooph., p. 474, plate 38, fig.

--- SENTENCE 2306 ---
Madrepora gracilis Dana, U. S. Expl.

--- SENTENCE 2307 ---
Exped., Zooph., p. 482, plate 41, fig.

--- SENTENCE 2308 ---
Madrepora formosa Brook, Cat.

--- SENTENCE 2309 ---
Genus Mad., p. 43.

--- SENTENCE 2310 ---
The Samoan collection contains a large suite of specimens belonging to this species, and although they show considerable variation, they are undoubtedly specifically alike.

--- SENTENCE 2311 ---
The two main directions in which variation occurs is in the thickness of the stems and the shape of the calicular openings.

--- SENTENCE 2312 ---
Brook recognized the minor importance of the diameter of the branches and combined A. brachiata with A. formosa.

--- SENTENCE 2313 ---
Dana's original specimens of these two

species are in the U. S. National Museum and show another difference besides the thickness of the branches.

--- SENTENCE 2314 ---
This difference lies in the calicular openings of the radial corallites.

--- SENTENCE 2315 ---
In the majority of cases the apertures of the radial corallites of A. brachiata are oblique, while they are more often cut at right angles to the corallite in A. formosa.

--- SENTENCE 2316 ---
This is so variable, however, that I am leaving the two combined.

--- SENTENCE 2317 ---
To typical A. formosa and the brachiata variety I wish to add another variety, var.

--- SENTENCE 2318 ---
Dana's type of A. gracilis is No. 333, U. S. National Museum.

--- SENTENCE 2319 ---
It is nearly exactly like A. formosa, with the exception that many of its radial corallites are truncated obliquely and thus have elliptical openings.

--- SENTENCE 2320 ---
Many others, however, have circular apertures, as in typical A. formosa.

--- SENTENCE 2321 ---
Specimen No. 265, U. S. National Museum, was marked by Dana Madrepora formosa?.

--- SENTENCE 2322 ---
So many of its corallites have oblique apertures that Dana hesitated in placing it with this species.

--- SENTENCE 2323 ---
In the Samoan suite there is also wide variation in this respect on the same colonies.

--- SENTENCE 2324 ---
I believe, therefore, that A. gracilis is only a variety of A. formosa with oblique apertures predominating.

--- SENTENCE 2325 ---
The type of A. brachiata, No. 295, U. S. National Museum, resembles the type of A. gracilis more than A. formosa because of its frequent obliquely truncated radial corallites.

--- SENTENCE 2326 ---
As the gradation from variety gracilis to variety brachiata is so gradual, I have had to choose a purely arbitrary boundary.

--- SENTENCE 2327 ---
I have called all specimens gracilis whose branches are 12 mm.

--- SENTENCE 2328 ---
or less in diameter and the larger specimens brachiata.

--- SENTENCE 2329 ---
This procedure may be criticized, but I believe it good policy to subdivide a species where the variation is considerable, in order to give a clearer picture of the species.

--- SENTENCE 2330 ---
I am referring all of the Samoa specimens to the varieties gracilis and brachiata.

--- SENTENCE 2331 ---
In all of them corallites with elliptical apertures predominate over those with circu-

lar openings, especially on the higher parts of the branches.

--- SENTENCE 2332 ---
In the gracilis variety the branches are 6 to 12 mm. in diameter.

--- SENTENCE 2333 ---
They are spreading-ramose and attenuate.

--- SENTENCE 2334 ---
The axial corallites vary from 1.5 to 2.5 mm.

--- SENTENCE 2335 ---
in diameter including the walls, and average 2 mm. long.

--- SENTENCE 2336 ---
There is generally a cycle of 6 well-developed septa with a cycle of less-developed intermediate ones.

--- SENTENCE 2337 ---
The radial corallites are usually from 2 to 3 mm.

--- SENTENCE 2338 ---
long on the upper parts of the branches, and become very short on the basal parts of the stems.

--- SENTENCE 2339 ---
In between the well-developed ones are closely crowded smaller corallites all over the corallum.

--- SENTENCE 2340 ---
The corallites are tubo-nariform; the outer sides very slightly thicker than the inner; the tips truncated obliquely.

--- SENTENCE 2341 ---
Septa best developed when calicular walls are thickest.

--- SENTENCE 2342 ---
This is usually the case on the basal parts of the branches.

--- SENTENCE 2343 ---
One cycle generally fairly well developed with irregularly developed intermediate septa.

--- SENTENCE 2344 ---
In one specimen the septa are poorly developed in nearly all the calices.

--- SENTENCE 2345 ---
The two directives generally prominent.

--- SENTENCE 2346 ---
The sides of the corallites are distinctly striate, with fine granulations on striations.

--- SENTENCE 2347 ---
Between striations walls

are perforate.

--- SENTENCE 2348 ---
In one specimen the under side of the corallum has very poorly developed coral-

lites, even far up on the branches.

--- SENTENCE 2349 ---
Variety gracilis grades gently into the brachiata variety.

--- SENTENCE 2350 ---
The latter has longer and stouter branches which thin out more abruptly, and which perhaps do not branch quite so profusely.

--- SENTENCE 2351 ---
The calices are slightly longer, with thicker walls and correspondingly better developed septa.

--- SENTENCE 2352 ---
One distinctive character common to the whole species is the crowded appearance of the septa due to the large number of small septa taking up the space between

the larger.

--- SENTENCE 2353 ---
This is most pronounced on the basal portions.

--- SENTENCE 2354 ---
Von Marenzeller's Acropora eminens appears to be very close to this species.

--- SENTENCE 2355 ---
Stations, Pago Pago Harbor, Tutuila, Samoa:

Variety gracilis.

--- SENTENCE 2428 ---

Distribution.—Fiji Islands; Great Barrier Reef; Samoa Islands.

#### Acropora teres (Verrill).

--- SENTENCE 2429 ---

--- SENTENCE 2430 ---

19, a, b.

1866.

--- SENTENCE 2432 ---

--- SENTENCE 2434 ---
Verrill's type of Acropora teres from Ousima is No. 377, U. S. National Museum.

--- SENTENCE 2435 ---
It appears to be much worn and only the lower part of the branches remains.

--- SENTENCE 2436 ---
The Samoan specimen is a large, arborescently branched one, with the branches laxly dividing.

--- SENTENCE 2437 ---
The branches are long, round, slightly tapering; the largest is 20 cm.

--- SENTENCE 2438 ---
long and 12 mm.

--- SENTENCE 2439 ---
in diameter at the base.

--- SENTENCE 2440 ---
The branchlets are slender, more rapidly tapering, with apices generally rounded.

--- SENTENCE 2441 ---
The axial corallites are 2.5 to 3 mm. in diameter.

--- SENTENCE 2442 ---
At times they are scarcely exsert and again they may be exsert as much as 3 mm.

--- SENTENCE 2443 ---
Wall usually rounded and porous; 6 fairly prominent septa of first cycle alternate with small intermediates.

--- SENTENCE 2444 ---
Where exsert the wall is strongly costulate.

--- SENTENCE 2445 ---

--- SENTENCE 2446 ---
in diameter; average 0.75 to 1 mm.; numerous in places and at others more scattered, as in type.

--- SENTENCE 2447 ---
Those on the branchlets and on the upper part of the corallum are provided with thin, labellate lower lips which are either slightly ascending or spreading perpendicular to stem.

--- SENTENCE 2448 ---
Slightly farther down they become immersed; by far the major part of the corallum possesses immersed corallites.

--- SENTENCE 2449 ---
The septa are very poorly developed, with generally only the upper directive visible.

--- SENTENCE 2450 ---
The coenenchyma is vermiculate, sharply scabrous, and possessed of fine granulations.

--- SENTENCE 2451 ---
The radial corallites average slightly smaller than those of the type and in places are more crowded.

--- SENTENCE 2452 ---
In the type they are placed about 1.5 mm. from each other, a distance which is also often observable in the Samoan specimens.

--- SENTENCE 2453 ---
Verrill likens this species to A. virgata (Dana), but the resemblance is only superficial.

--- SENTENCE 2454 ---
A. cribripora (Dana), the type of which is No. 289, U. S. National Museum, is very close to this species.

--- SENTENCE 2455 ---
I am keeping them separate until more specimens are available, mainly because of the large, more crowded radial corallites of the former.

--- SENTENCE 2456 ---
The specimens which Brook referred to A. cribripora certainly do not belong there.

--- SENTENCE 2457 ---
The species is peculiar for its lack of septa in its radial corallites (usually only the upper directive is present), whereas Brook's specimens contain 6.

#### Acropora nobilis (Dana.)

--- SENTENCE 2463 ---
Plate 11, fig. 1; fig.

--- SENTENCE 2464 ---
2 shows part of Dana's type of A. secunda, No. 323, U. S. N. M.

1846.

--- SENTENCE 2465 ---
Madrepora nobilis Dana, U. S. Expl.

--- SENTENCE 2466 ---
Exped., Zooph., p. 481, plate 40, fig.

--- SENTENCE 2467 ---
Madrepora nobilis Brook, Cat.

--- SENTENCE 2468 ---
Genus Mad., p. 135.

1902.

--- SENTENCE 2469 ---
Acropora nobilis Verrill (typical), Corals of the Cenus Acrop., p. 233.

--- SENTENCE 2470 ---
1, p. 40) stated that he had carefully examined the types of A. nobilis and A. secunda and had come to the conclusion that the latter should be regarded simply as a variety of the former.

--- SENTENCE 2471 ---
Quelch (Challenger Reef Corals, p. 150) followed Verrill and referred to A. nobilis some specimens which closely fitted Dana's description of his A. secunda.

--- SENTENCE 2472 ---
He, however, questioned the correctness of this.

--- SENTENCE 2473 ---
Brook (op. cit., p. 135) kept the two species separate.

--- SENTENCE 2474 ---
Verrill in his "Corals of the Genus Acropora," page 233, defended his stand and suggested that Brook's specimens of A. secunda were not secunda, but belonged to a new species.

--- SENTENCE 2475 ---
He proposed the name secundella for this new species.

--- SENTENCE 2476 ---
Dana's types of A. nobilis and A. secunda are in the U. S. National Museum.

--- SENTENCE 2477 ---
Verrill may possibly be correct in combining these two species, and yet the types are so distinct that I am inclined to keep them apart.

--- SENTENCE 2478 ---
A. nobilis is a much larger, sturdier species with stems many times thicker.

--- SENTENCE 2479 ---
In A. nobilis the radial corallites are very unequal in size.

--- SENTENCE 2480 ---
Some are large dimidiate, others smaller, labellate, with a thin wall, and others immersed.

--- SENTENCE 2481 ---
In A. secunda their sizes are much more constant.

--- SENTENCE 2482 ---
The axial corallites of the latter may be exsert 3 or 4 mm., whereas in the other species they are in general not so prominent.

--- SENTENCE 2483 ---
The radial corallites of A. secunda are more appressed than those of A. nobilis, which often stand perpendicular to the branch.

--- SENTENCE 2484 ---
The coenenchyma of the latter is much more spongy-reticulate than that of the former.

--- SENTENCE 2485 ---
It is hard to say without a good photograph whether Brook's A. secunda is or is not a different species, but I am inclined to believe that his description does not come too far from Dana's type (No. 323, U. S. Nat.

--- SENTENCE 2486 ---
It is of course wrong to place it in a different subgenus than A. nobilis, as he has done.

--- SENTENCE 2487 ---
The Samoan collection contains one large branch with several smaller ones, which have probably been broken from the same corallum.

--- SENTENCE 2488 ---
The large branch is nearly as much as 4 cm.

--- SENTENCE 2489 ---
One curious feature concerning this specimen is that the radial corallites make no pretense at similarity of position.

--- SENTENCE 2490 ---
They face in all directions.

--- SENTENCE 2491 ---
That is to say, the thick lip of one corallite may face the proximal

end of the branch, while that of its nearest neighbor may be turned at any angle to it, and, indeed, may be completely reversed.

--- SENTENCE 2492 ---
This characteristic is most common on the lower portions of the branch.

--- SENTENCE 2493 ---
Station.—Pago Pago Harbor, Tutuila, Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 2494 ---
Rough water, 10 feet deep.

--- SENTENCE 2495 ---
Distribution.—Singapore; Ceylon; Java; Samoa.

#### Acropora samoensis (Brook).

--- SENTENCE 2496 ---

--- SENTENCE 2497 ---
4 shows Dana's type, No. 332, of A. humilis.

--- SENTENCE 2498 ---
Madrepora samoensis Brook, Cat.

--- SENTENCE 2499 ---
Genus Mad., p. 143, plate 6, fig.

--- SENTENCE 2500 ---
c; plate 31, fig.

--- SENTENCE 2501 ---
Madrepora samoensis Studer, Zool.

--- SENTENCE 2502 ---
Dr. Mayor collected 5 beautiful examples of this species.

--- SENTENCE 2503 ---
All possess a rounded, incrusting base.

--- SENTENCE 2504 ---
They are not as large as Brook's described type; the largest measures about 12 cm.

--- SENTENCE 2505 ---
high and 16 cm. broad.

--- SENTENCE 2506 ---
The branching is very irregular.

--- SENTENCE 2507 ---
At times the branches increase in length towards the center of the corallum and in other specimens they become shorter at the center.

--- SENTENCE 2508 ---
In the latter case they are 2 to 4 cm.

--- SENTENCE 2509 ---
long at the center and as much as 9 cm.

--- SENTENCE 2510 ---
long around the edge; the dimensions are reversed in the other case.

--- SENTENCE 2511 ---
Brook's description is very good.

--- SENTENCE 2512 ---
Dana's *Madrepora humilis*, the type of which is No. 332, U. S. National Museum, has a growth-form very similar to this species.

--- SENTENCE 2513 ---
At first glance it is difficult to distinguish any differences between them.

--- SENTENCE 2514 ---
The two main differences, however, are:

(1) The space in between the large radial corallites is taken up by numerous small, subimmersed or immersed ones in A. samoensis.

--- SENTENCE 2515 ---
This is never the case in A. humilis, which has radial corallites of nearly the same size.

--- SENTENCE 2516 ---
(2) The walls and the coenenchyma of A. samoensis are much more porous and delicate than those of A. humilis, the texture of which is rather firm.

--- SENTENCE 2517 ---
Dana's illustrations do not give a correct idea of A. humilis.

--- SENTENCE 2518 ---
They show the radial corallites as being appressed and rather isolated.

--- SENTENCE 2519 ---
This is not at all the case, as the corallites are arranged exactly as the large ones of A. samoensis.

--- SENTENCE 2520 ---
There seems to be no doubt that the specimens which Brook placed with A. humilis belong to some other species.

--- SENTENCE 2521 ---
It is only natural that Brook should have made this mistake because of Dana's poor illustrations.

--- SENTENCE 2522 ---
Plate 11, figure 4, shows Dana's type of A. humilis.

--- SENTENCE 2523 ---
A specimen illustrated in Dr. Mayor's paper "Growth-Rate of Samoan Corals," plate 4, figs. 11 a, b, appears to belong to A. humilis.

--- SENTENCE 2524 ---
I have hesitated from definitely placing it there because of its stouter branchlets.

Nos.

#### Acropora valida (Dana).

--- SENTENCE 2532 ---
1a (type No. 272 U. S. N. M.), 1b, 1c.

--- SENTENCE 2533 ---
See also Mayor's "Growth-Rate of Samoan Corals."

1846.

--- SENTENCE 2534 ---
Madrepora valida Dana, U. S. Expl.

--- SENTENCE 2535 ---
Exped., Zooph., p. 461, plate 35, fig.

--- SENTENCE 2536 ---
Madrepora valida Brook, Cat.

--- SENTENCE 2537 ---
Genus Mad., p. 168.

--- SENTENCE 2538 ---
The following is a description of this species:

--- SENTENCE 2539 ---
Corallum cespitose, measuring 7 cm.

--- SENTENCE 2540 ---
in height, 12 cm.

--- SENTENCE 2541 ---
in width and 5 cm.

--- SENTENCE 2542 ---
The branches are 10 to 16 mm. thick at the base and keep approximately the same diameter throughout their length.

--- SENTENCE 2543 ---
Apices of branches from 2 to 2.5 cm.

--- SENTENCE 2544 ---
Near the top each branch usually sends off two or more short, stout branchlets.

--- SENTENCE 2545 ---
The axial corallites are 2.5 to 3 mm.

--- SENTENCE 2546 ---
across, the apertures measure about 0.75 mm.

--- SENTENCE 2547 ---
in diameter, exsert 1.5 to 2 mm.

--- SENTENCE 2548 ---
Six well-developed septa usually meet

in the center of the calice; secondaries less developed.

--- SENTENCE 2549 ---
Radial corallites large, stout, unequal, and appressed tubiform.

--- SENTENCE 2550 ---
They are truncated obliquely and thus have elliptical apertures.

--- SENTENCE 2551 ---
Walls thick, especially on outer side, and rounded.

--- SENTENCE 2552 ---
Length of larger corallites 3 to 8 mm., diameter averages 2 mm., few immersed on basal parts of corallum.

--- SENTENCE 2553 ---
First cycle of septa fairly well developed, with directives very prominent.

--- SENTENCE 2554 ---
Under surface of outer branches flattened and nearly naked.

--- SENTENCE 2555 ---
Walls of septa covered with very fine echinulations, which at times run in rows.

--- SENTENCE 2556 ---
There are 5 specimens of this species in the collection.

--- SENTENCE 2557 ---
No. 1 is a large specimen with branches 7 cm.

--- SENTENCE 2558 ---
long and much more even than in Dana's type.

--- SENTENCE 2559 ---
No. 2 is the one described, and shown on plate 12, fig.

--- SENTENCE 2560 ---
No. 3 varies somewhat from the others by its thicker corallite walls.

--- SENTENCE 2561 ---
This species resembles A. variabilis Klunzinger, but is separated from it mainly by the character of the radial corallites.

--- SENTENCE 2562 ---
In A. variabilis they are tubular, with small, circular apertures, and walls generally equally thick all around the openings.

--- SENTENCE 2563 ---
In A. valida the inner wall is usually much thinner than the outer, the apertures elliptical, the corallites often having a nariform appearance.

--- SENTENCE 2564 ---

#### Acropora canaliculata (Klunzinger).

--- SENTENCE 2576 ---
Madrepora canaliculata Klunzinger, Korallthiere des Rothen Meeres, part 2, p. 12, plate 1, fig. 3; plate 4, fig. 10; plate 9, fig.

--- SENTENCE 2577 ---
Von Marenzeller (1906, Riffkorallen des Rothen Meeres) thinks it very probable that this species should be referred to the synonymy of A. scherzeriana Brueggemann.

--- SENTENCE 2578 ---
He says the plates of Klunzinger's work do not give an exact conception of the species as it is represented in the Berlin Museum example 2129.

--- SENTENCE 2579 ---
He likens the latter example in growth-form to his No. 15649 (Taf. 12, fig. 27) of Acropora scherzeriana, which has longer and more slender branches than A. canaliculata represented by Klunzinger.

--- SENTENCE 2580 ---
The lone specimen collected by Dr. Mayor corresponds very well in form of growth with von Marenzeller's specimen No. 15649, except that it has somewhat shorter branches, and must therefore be close to the Berlin Museum example 2129.

--- SENTENCE 2581 ---
In the U. S. National Museum there are several specimens of Acropora canaliculata from Paumotus, and also some specimens of A. scherzeriana from Cocos-Keeling described and pictured by Vaughan in Carnegie Institution of Washington Publication 213.

--- SENTENCE 2582 ---
The Fiji Island specimen collected by Dr. Mayor corresponds to those from Paumotus in all respects except that its branches are more slender and longer.

--- SENTENCE 2583 ---
I am keeping the species separate until more examples can be studied, although I believe probably they should be united.

Station.—Suva, Fiji.

--- SENTENCE 2584 ---
Barrier reef, shallow water.

--- SENTENCE 2585 ---
Distribution.—Red Sea; Paumotus; Fiji Islands.

#### Acropora fructicosa (Brook).

1893.

--- SENTENCE 2586 ---
Madrepora fructicosa Brook, Cat.

--- SENTENCE 2587 ---
Genus Mad., p. 138, plate 18, fig.

--- SENTENCE 2588 ---
A.

One good-sized colony and a small fragment of this beautiful and very regular species were collected by Dr. Mayor.

--- SENTENCE 2589 ---
He used the large specimen for a growth experiment.

--- SENTENCE 2590 ---
It was removed from a position 600 feet from shore on the Aua Reef flat.

--- SENTENCE 2591 ---
and one half of it was grown in a highly agitated, breaker-washed location on the edge of a reef-patch, while the other half was planted in water 42 feet deep.

--- SENTENCE 2592 ---
The shallow-water part thrived, while the deep water half died without growing.

--- SENTENCE 2593 ---
It can be noticed that the rough-water half has had its branches slightly stunted because of its location.

--- SENTENCE 2594 ---
The latter half fits Brook's description and plate of M. fructicosa perfectly, except that it is not so large.

--- SENTENCE 2595 ---
It measures 6 cm.

--- SENTENCE 2596 ---
in height and 15 cm.

--- SENTENCE 2597 ---
This species is very similar to A. acervata (Dana), the type of which is No. 271, U. S. National Museum.

--- SENTENCE 2598 ---
I have kept them separate because I find that the radial corallites of A. fructicosa are perhaps more regular, and the septa are not as well developed.

--- SENTENCE 2599 ---
In A. acervata the primary septa are all well developed and the secondaries fairly so.

--- SENTENCE 2600 ---
In A. fructicosa generally only the directives are well developed, the other primaries very irregular, and the secondaries often entirely missing.

--- SENTENCE 2601 ---
In the corallites towards the ends of the branches frequently only the directives can be seen.

--- SENTENCE 2602 ---
Brook's description and illustration are very good.

--- SENTENCE 2603 ---
Stations, Pago Pago Harbor, Tutuila, Samoa:

--- SENTENCE 2604 ---
4s and 4D (Mayor).

#### Acropora corymbosa (Lamarck).

--- SENTENCE 2611 ---
Also Mayor's "Growth-Rate of Samoan Corals."

1893.

--- SENTENCE 2612 ---
Acropora corymbosa Brook, Cat.

--- SENTENCE 2613 ---
Gen. Mad., p. 97.
1906.

--- SENTENCE 2614 ---
Acropora corymbosa von Marenzeller, Denksch.

--- SENTENCE 2615 ---
Wien, vol. 80, p. 32, plates 1, 2, 3. 1918.

--- SENTENCE 2616 ---
Acropora corymbosa Vaughan, Carnegie Inst.

--- SENTENCE 2617 ---
Wash. Pub. 213, p. 171, plate 67, fig.

--- SENTENCE 2618 ---
Corallum corymbose, flattened on top.

--- SENTENCE 2619 ---
Branches projecting upward, marginal ones obliquely; those in center of corallum 35 mm.

--- SENTENCE 2620 ---
long, marginal ones 65 mm; Diameter at base of simple branchlet 5 or 6 mm.

--- SENTENCE 2621 ---
proliferous corallites with buds (making minute branchlets) frequent near apices of larger branchlets.

--- SENTENCE 2622 ---
Axial corallites 1.5 to 2 mm. in diameter; project about 1.5 mm.; walls fragile, costulate, very porous; fossæ wider than thickness of walls; six primary septa fairly

well developed, secondaries scarcely even visible.

--- SENTENCE 2623 ---
The radial corallites are rather numerous and fairly regularly placed; usually labellate; ascending slightly less than 45°.

--- SENTENCE 2624 ---
The walls are very fragile and porous, with distinct costæ.

--- SENTENCE 2625 ---
Diameter of corallites 1 to 1.5 mm., length up to 3 mm.

--- SENTENCE 2626 ---
Septa very thin; only directives can be faintly made out in distal corallites; in those closer to the base the 6 primaries can be seen with obscure secondaries.

--- SENTENCE 2627 ---
Apertures generally subcircular, often distinctly elliptical; circular in immersed or subimmersed corallites around basal portions.

--- SENTENCE 2628 ---
Numerous long tubular corallites with buds on underside of marginal branches.

--- SENTENCE 2629 ---
I have described one of seven examples in the Samoan collection.

--- SENTENCE 2630 ---
The others are very similar and vary mainly in the thickness of the branches.

--- SENTENCE 2631 ---
In No. 5 the

base of the simple branchlets may be 8 to 10 mm. in diameter.

--- SENTENCE 2632 ---
These examples coincide with a specimen identified by Vaughan from Tahiti in the U. S. National Museum, and also with one collected by Dr. Fred.

--- SENTENCE 2633 ---
Baker at Fanning Island, which was referred to by Vaughan in the above-mentioned paper.

--- SENTENCE 2634 ---
They are similar to the specimens represented by von Marenzeller in his figures 7 and 7a of plate 3; his figure 6a represents the characteristics of some of the branchlets fairly well, although as a rule the Samoan branchlets are more tapering.

#### Acropora cymbicyathus (Brook).

--- SENTENCE 2642 ---
Also illustrated in Mayor's "Growth-Rate of Samoan Corals."

--- SENTENCE 2643 ---

--- SENTENCE 2644 ---

--- SENTENCE 2645 ---
They average 2 mm.

--- SENTENCE 2646 ---
in diameter, whereas Brook's specimens average 2.5 mm.

--- SENTENCE 2647 ---

mm., and Brook describes them as being 2 mm. exsert.

--- SENTENCE 2648 ---
This species is very similar to Acropora nasuta (Dana), the type of which is No. 260, U. S. National Museum.

--- SENTENCE 2649 ---
As a rule, the walls of the radial corallites are thinner and more fragile in the latter species.

--- SENTENCE 2650 ---
Verrill (1902, Corals of the Genus Acropora, p. 257) reports one of Dana's types of nasuta in the museum of Yale University as belonging to the variety crassilabia of Brook.

--- SENTENCE 2651 ---
This variety has decidedly thick walls.

--- SENTENCE 2652 ---
Thus there is a good deal of variation in this respect within the species.

--- SENTENCE 2653 ---
Usually the septa are better developed in A. nasuta, but this also is subject to variation.

--- SENTENCE 2654 ---
The main feature which distinguishes the two species is that the radial corallites of A. cymbicyathus are more appressed.

--- SENTENCE 2655 ---
The distal ones stand at an angle of 30° to 45° to the branch, and lower down they are even more appressed.

--- SENTENCE 2656 ---
They frequently spread out at nearly 90° in the other species.

#### Acropora hyacinthus (Dana).

--- SENTENCE 2663 ---
Plate 13, fig. 3; plate 14, figs.

--- SENTENCE 2664 ---
Also well illustrated in Dr. Mayor's "Growth-Rate of Samoan Corals."

1846.

--- SENTENCE 2665 ---
Madrepora hyacinthus Dana, U. S. Expl.

--- SENTENCE 2666 ---
Zooph., p. 444, plate 32, fig.

--- SENTENCE 2667 ---
Madrepora arcuata Brook, Cat.

--- SENTENCE 2668 ---
p. 102, plate 12.

1901.

--- SENTENCE 2669 ---
Madrepora arcuata Studer, Zool.

--- SENTENCE 2670 ---
Jahrb., 5 Heft, p. 395.

--- SENTENCE 2671 ---
Dana's plate 32, figure 2, shows simply a fragment of the type specimen, No. 246, U. S. National Museum, and gives no true idea of the characteristics of the species.

--- SENTENCE 2672 ---
Plate 13, figure 3, of this paper is a full-sized photograph of the type and gives a better conception of it.

--- SENTENCE 2673 ---
Dr. Mayor collected a splendid suite of 16 examples of this species, which illustrates the wide variation exceedingly well.

--- SENTENCE 2674 ---
The specimens vary a good deal in the size and shape of the corallum and in the thickness of the branchlets.

--- SENTENCE 2675 ---
The largest specimen (No. 3) measures 33 cm.

--- SENTENCE 2676 ---
in diameter and 14 cm.

--- SENTENCE 2677 ---
in height; the smallest is 5 cm.

--- SENTENCE 2678 ---
wide and 6 cm.

--- SENTENCE 2679 ---
The large specimens are all either flabellate or vasiform, extending from a round pedicel;

the smaller ones are arborescent, cespitose, or subhemispherical.

--- SENTENCE 2680 ---
From the study of the specimens I am led to believe that when young the branches grow in such a way as to give the corallum a cespitose, bushy shape.

--- SENTENCE 2681 ---
At this stage the branchlets with their corallites are just as numerous on the underside of the peripheral branches as on the upper.

--- SENTENCE 2682 ---
As the colony gets older the peripheral branches grow rapidly in an upward oblique direction, while those in the center cease to grow.

--- SENTENCE 2683 ---
Thus what was at one time the convex portion of the corallum now becomes concave.

--- SENTENCE 2684 ---
As further growth occurs the peripheral branches fuse with each other to form a more or less perfect platform, and on the underside the branchlets with their corallites become so appressed to the main, fused branches that the under surface is comparatively smooth.

--- SENTENCE 2685 ---
On the upper surface the branchlets grow upwards with increasing angle from the margin towards the center of the corallum, where they are nearly perpendicular to the platform.

--- SENTENCE 2686 ---
This method of growth is shown by plate 2, figs.

--- SENTENCE 2687 ---
1a, 1b, of Dr. Mayor's paper on "Growth-Rate of Samoan Corals."

--- SENTENCE 2688 ---
Plate 5, figure 14a, shows specimen No. 1=No. 14 (Mayor) on April 10, 1917.

--- SENTENCE 2689 ---
Figure 1c of plate 14 of the present paper shows the same specimen on the date it was collected, June 11, 1918.

--- SENTENCE 2690 ---
Figure 1a shows one of the young specimens (No. 10) in the collection and figure 1b shows a slightly older one in which the corallum is turning from the cespitose to the vasiform stage.

--- SENTENCE 2691 ---
Several other figures in Dr. Mayor's paper illustrate the species.

--- SENTENCE 2692 ---
Dana's type represents a specimen which has turned from the cespitose stage into the vasiform stage, but whose branchlets of the underside of the peripheral branches, which are just in the act of fusing, have not as yet become appressed.

--- SENTENCE 2693 ---
The Samoan specimens illustrate every stage of growth.

--- SENTENCE 2694 ---
It is perfectly clear that Brook's type of *M. arcuata* is a fully developed example of *A. hyacinthus*.

--- SENTENCE 2695 ---
Two of the specimens (Nos. 1 and 2) of this collection fit Brook's excellent description and illustration of *A. arcuata* perfectly.

--- SENTENCE 2696 ---
No. 3 shows a variation in the thickness of the branchlets; those at the margin measure about 4 mm., while towards the center they may be as much as 6 mm. in diameter.

--- SENTENCE 2697 ---
In No. 4 the branchlets average from 4 to 5 or 6 mm. in thickness.

--- SENTENCE 2698 ---
This corresponds with Dana's type.

--- SENTENCE 2699 ---
On Nos. 1 and 2 the branchlets are thinner and average 4 mm., as do those of Brook's type of *A. arcuata*.

--- SENTENCE 2700 ---
The corallite walls in all the specimens are porous.

--- SENTENCE 2701 ---
There is a specimen of this species in the U. S. National Museum from the

Philippines.

#### Acropora prolixa (Verrill).

--- SENTENCE 2753 ---
1a, 1b, from Samoa; 1c, type No. 414 U. S. Nat.

--- SENTENCE 2754 ---
Mus.; 1d, Dana's type of A. echinata from the Sulu Sea, No. 276 U. S. Nat.

--- SENTENCE 2755 ---
Madrepora prolixa Verrill, Communications Essex Inst., Salem, vol. 5, p. 22.

1902.

--- SENTENCE 2756 ---
Acropora prolixa Verrill, Trans. Conn.

--- SENTENCE 2757 ---
Sci., vol. 11, p. 237, plate 36, figs.

--- SENTENCE 2758 ---
3. 3a; plate 36A. figs.

--- SENTENCE 2759 ---
3. 3a; 36F, fig.

--- SENTENCE 2760 ---
The collection contains one good-sized specimen and a fragment of this species.

--- SENTENCE 2761 ---
The corallum of the former is erect, arborescent, reaching height of 15 cm.

--- SENTENCE 2762 ---
The branchlets possess numerous long, slender, tubular, proliferous corallites which have shorter radial corallites extending from them.

--- SENTENCE 2763 ---
In the terminal portions of the corallum the axial corallites have several short, appressed radial corallites below them.

--- SENTENCE 2764 ---
The average diameter of the normal radial corallite is 1.25 mm.

--- SENTENCE 2765 ---
They may be as much as 1 cm.

--- SENTENCE 2766 ---
long, but probably average about 6 mm., not including the appressed ones, which of course are much shorter.

--- SENTENCE 2767 ---
They are usually strongly contracted at the aperture, although this is not true in many cases, especially near the top of the corallum.

--- SENTENCE 2768 ---
Six distinct septa are visible; very seldom are more distinguishable.

--- SENTENCE 2769 ---
The coenenchyma and the walls of the corallites are covered with fine granulations which are generally arranged to form striations, but in some parts, however, the striations are indistinct.

--- SENTENCE 2770 ---
Immersed corallites are scattered over the branches, although on the largest branch not a single one appears for a distance of 5 cm.

--- SENTENCE 2771 ---
Verrill's type of *Madrepora prolixa* is No. 414, U. S. National Museum.

--- SENTENCE 2772 ---
The Samoan specimens agree with it in every way.

--- SENTENCE 2773 ---
Genus *Madrepora*, p. 187) doubtfully placed *M. prolixa* with *M. longicyathus* Milne Edwards and Haime.

--- SENTENCE 2774 ---
They are certainly very much alike, but since Verrill had the opportunity of comparing his species with a part of the type of *M. longicyathus* and pronounced them different, we will certainly have to consider them so until we can

get more proof to the contrary than Brook had.

--- SENTENCE 2775 ---
One of Dana's types of A. echinata is from the Sulu Seas and is No. 276, U. S. National Museum.

--- SENTENCE 2776 ---
I can see no difference between it and Verrill's type of A. prolixa.

--- SENTENCE 2777 ---
It is not the one which Dana described and figured.

--- SENTENCE 2778 ---
The latter is No. 275, U. S. National Museum, and is illustrated by Vaughan (Bull. 59, U. S. National Museum, 1907, plates 49 and 50).

--- SENTENCE 2779 ---
I believe that the Sulu Sea specimen really belongs to A. prolixa, as there is quite a difference between it and the described type.

--- SENTENCE 2780 ---
It is barely possible that A. prolixa belongs to the synonymy of A. echinata, but I doubt it very much.

--- SENTENCE 2781 ---
Typical echinata is not a quiet-water form and prolixa an agitated-water form, as one might conjecture.

--- SENTENCE 2782 ---
Of the two Samoan specimens, one was grown in rough water and the other in deep, quiet water, and still there is no difference between them.

--- SENTENCE 2783 ---
A. rayneri (Brook) appears to be so near to prolixa that it is doubtful whether the two should be separated.

#### Acropora africana (Brook).

--- SENTENCE 2798 ---

--- SENTENCE 2799 ---
B.

One specimen of this distinctive species was collected by Dr. Mayor.

--- SENTENCE 2800 ---
It corresponds to Brook's description and plate in nearly every detail.

--- SENTENCE 2801 ---
It differs in that the diameter of the axial corallites averages from 2.25 to 3 mm.

--- SENTENCE 2802 ---
instead of from 3 to 3.5 mm., and that the septa in the immersed corallites are not quite so well developed as he describes them.

--- SENTENCE 2803 ---
Brook says that the arrangement of the radial corallites is very irregular.

--- SENTENCE 2804 ---
I find this to be the case on the branches of the same size as the type (3 to 3.5 cm.), but on the larger branches there is a tendency for the larger corallites to group themselves in fairly regular longitudinal rows.

--- SENTENCE 2805 ---
One of the outstanding features of the species is the large, open, radial corallites,

nearly devoid of septa, except for the poorly developed directives.

#### Acropora leptocyathus (Brook).

--- SENTENCE 2809 ---
Also well illustrated in Mayor's "Growth-Rate of Samoan Corals."

1893.

--- SENTENCE 2810 ---
Madrepora leptocyathus Brook, Cat. Gen. Mad., p. 159, plate 16, fig.

--- SENTENCE 2811 ---
This species shows many variations in the different specimens belonging to it collected from Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 2812 ---
They are all agreed, however, as regards the following major characteristics:

(1) Broad, flat base.

--- SENTENCE 2813 ---
(2) Rather short branches usually simple, rising erect in the center of the corallum, and branching obliquely outward towards the periphery.

--- SENTENCE 2814 ---
All rise to about the same level in the same specimen.

--- SENTENCE 2815 ---
(3) Apices of branches same distance apart in all specimens, i. e., usually 1.7 cm.

(4) Axial corallites 2 to 3 mm. in diameter, scarcely exsert.

--- SENTENCE 2816 ---
(5) Radial corallites crowded, somewhat unequal.

--- SENTENCE 2817 ---
Numerous immersed ones on basal plate.

--- SENTENCE 2818 ---
(6) Corallum rather dense; surface finely reticulate and echinulate; walls striato-echinulate.

--- SENTENCE 2819 ---
I am referring 12 specimens to this species.

--- SENTENCE 2820 ---
They vary among themselves in respect to the thickness and length of the branches and the character of the radial corallites.

--- SENTENCE 2821 ---
No. 1 has rather thick, short branches (1.5 cm.

--- SENTENCE 2822 ---
diameter at base and 2.5 cm. high).

--- SENTENCE 2823 ---
The branches are angular at their bases, but become conical higher up.

--- SENTENCE 2824 ---
The crowded radial corallites appear rather even because of the numerous large ones.

--- SENTENCE 2825 ---
Closer examination, however, shows many smaller ones of various lengths crowded in between the large corallites.

--- SENTENCE 2826 ---
Diameter of larger ones 1.5 mm.; tubular with oblique apertures; walls slightly thicker than in other specimens.

--- SENTENCE 2827 ---
12 septa imperfectly made out; directives prominent.

--- SENTENCE 2828 ---
Whole corallum having a neat appearance.

--- SENTENCE 2829 ---
No. 6 has branches I to I.5 cm.

--- SENTENCE 2830 ---
in diameter and 2 to 3 cm.

--- SENTENCE 2831 ---
Large corallites stand out nearly at right angles to the branches; often 3 mm.

--- SENTENCE 2832 ---
long and I to I.5 mm. in diameter; not so numerous as in No.

--- SENTENCE 2833 ---
I, and this corallum has much rougher appearance.

--- SENTENCE 2834 ---
Larger corallites are tubo-nariform and trough-shaped;

--- SENTENCE 2835 ---
In No. 8 the branches are long and narrow, 1 cm.

--- SENTENCE 2836 ---
in diameter and 3 cm.

--- SENTENCE 2837 ---
in height, nearly same thickness towards ends as at basal portions.

--- SENTENCE 2838 ---
Radial corallites much shorter than in preceding (1.5 mm.); tubo-nariform; wall thin.

--- SENTENCE 2839 ---

of the walls but generally somewhat obliquely.

#### Acropora rotumana (Gardiner).

--- SENTENCE 2916 ---
Madrepora rotumana Gardiner, Proc. Zool.

--- SENTENCE 2917 ---
London, No. 17, p. 258, plate 23, fig.

--- SENTENCE 2918 ---
2

The two specimens of the Samoan collection correspond exactly to Gardiner's excellent description and illustration.

--- SENTENCE 2919 ---
They represent thick, horizontally growing branches which have fused to form broad plates, upon the upper surfaces of which are scattered conical elevations of various sizes.

--- SENTENCE 2920 ---
As Gardiner has said, these elevations are "really formed by the end twigs having turned upwards and having budded out fresh twigs at their bases."

--- SENTENCE 2921 ---
The undersurface contains numerous short, wart-like, subimmersed or immersed corallites, which, however, are not nearly so crowded as the corallites on the upper surface.

--- SENTENCE 2922 ---
As Gardiner has mentioned, this species is very similar to A. smithi (Brook) in the general appearance of the upper surface.

--- SENTENCE 2923 ---
It is, however, always pedicellate and never incrusting, as is the case in A. smithi.

--- SENTENCE 2924 ---
Both of these species resemble A. conigera (Dana) somewhat, but are distinct by their sharper-pointed elevations, their much more crowded and thicker-walled radial corallites, and better-developed

I believe it barely possible that A. rotumana and A. smithi belong to the same species.

--- SENTENCE 2925 ---
Stations.—Pago Pago Harbor, Tutuila, Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 2926 ---
Aua Reef; pure agitated water in shallow tide-pools near seaward edge.

--- SENTENCE 2927 ---
Distribution.—Rotuma; Samoa Island.

#### Acropora palifera (Lamarck).

--- SENTENCE 2928 ---
Madrepora palifera Brook, Cat. Gen. Mad., p. 131.

--- SENTENCE 2929 ---
Acropora palifera Vaughan, Carnegie Inst.

--- SENTENCE 2930 ---
Wash. Pub. 213, p. 178, plates 78 and 79; also plate 13, fig.

--- SENTENCE 2931 ---
The collection contains 5 examples of this species, which has been adequately described and figured by many authors.

--- SENTENCE 2932 ---
There is one point, however, that I wish to stress.

--- SENTENCE 2933 ---
I find that the variation in the development of the septa is very great.

--- SENTENCE 2934 ---
Dana's type of A. labrosa (No. 315, U. S. National Museum), which Brook referred to the synonymy of A. palifera (Lamarck), generally shows 6 well-developed and comparatively thick primaries with thinner secondaries.

--- SENTENCE 2935 ---
Some of the Murray Island specimens which Vaughan referred to this species show septa with the same degree of development as Dana's type, while others are very weakly developed and at times hardly distinguishable.

--- SENTENCE 2936 ---
In the Samoan specimens they are fairly well developed, but irregularly.

--- SENTENCE 2937 ---
No. 4 is evidently a fragment of the basal plate of a good-sized specimen, and contains no lobes.

--- SENTENCE 2938 ---
It possesses the wart-like corallites with smaller apertures characteristic of this portion of the corallum.

Stations, Pago Pago Harbor, Tutuila, Samoa:

Nos.

Shallow tide-pools on seaward edge of Utelei Reef.

--- SENTENCE 2940 ---

--- SENTENCE 2941 ---
Pure agitated water in shallow tide-pools near seaward edge of Aua Reef.
